Let f : ℝ d → ℝ ∪ { ∞ } f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\} be convex + and D ′ D^{\prime} be the set of its gradients (necessarily open within aff ⁡ D ′ \aff D^{\prime} ). Let 𝛍 ∈ D ′ \boldsymbol{\mu}\in D^{\prime} and let B B be a closed ball (in aff ⁡ D ′ \aff D^{\prime} ) centered at 𝛍 \boldsymbol{\mu} and fully contained in D ′ D^{\prime} . Then there exists a constant c c such that for all 𝛍 ′ ∈ B \boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}\in B H f ​ ( 𝝁 ′ ) ≃ ( 1 ± c ⁡ ∥ 𝝁 ′ − 𝝁 ∥ ) ​ H f ​ ( 𝝁 ) . H_{f}(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime})\simeq\bigl(1\pm c\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\mu}\rVert\bigr)H_{f}(\boldsymbol{\mu})\kern 5.0pt. Proof. Let g g be the function from Proposition A.9 . Similarly as we argued in the proof of Proposition A.9 , we can write H f ​ ( 𝝁 ) = ∇ 2 f ​ ( ∇ g ​ ( 𝝁 ) ) , H_{f}(\boldsymbol{\mu})=\nabla^{2}f(\nabla g(\boldsymbol{\mu}))\kern 5.0pt, because ∇ g ​ ( 𝝁 ) ∈ ∂ f ∗ ​ ( 𝝁 ) \nabla g(\boldsymbol{\mu})\in\partial f^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mu}) , and thus ∇ f ​ ( ∇ g ​ ( 𝝁 ) ) = 𝝁 \nabla f(\nabla g(\boldsymbol{\mu}))=\boldsymbol{\mu} . Since, g g has continuous second derivatives and f f has continuous third derivatives, the Mean Value Theorem implies that ∇ g \nabla g is Lipschitz continuous on any compact subset of D ′ D^{\prime} , and ∇ 2 f \nabla^{2}f is Lipschitz continuous on any compact subset of int ⁡ dom ⁡ f \interior\dom f . Since B B is compact, and so is its image under ∇ g \nabla g by continuity of ∇ g \nabla g , we obtain that both ∇ g \nabla g and ∇ 2 f \nabla^{2}f are Lipschitz on required sets, and so H f H_{f} is also Lipschitz within B B , with some constant L L , i.e., ∥ H f ​ ( 𝝁 ′ ) − H f ​ ( 𝝁 ) ∥ ≤ L ⁡ ∥ 𝝁 ′ − 𝝁 ∥ . \lVert H_{f}(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime})-H_{f}(\boldsymbol{\mu})\rVert\leq L\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\mu}\rVert\kern 5.0pt. Since range ⁡ ( H f ​ ( 𝝁 ′ ) ) = range ⁡ ( H f ​ ( 𝝁 ) ) = 𝒢 ⁡ ( f ) \range(H_{f}(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}))=\range(H_{f}(\boldsymbol{\mu}))=\mathcal{G}(f) , this implies that H f ​ ( 𝝁 ′ ) ≃ H f ​ ( 𝝁 ) ± L ⁡ ∥ 𝝁 ′ − 𝝁 ∥ ​ P , H_{f}(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime})\simeq H_{f}(\boldsymbol{\mu})\pm L\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\mu}\rVert P\kern 5.0pt, where P P is the projection on 𝒢 ⁡ ( f ) \mathcal{G}(f) . Since range ⁡ ( H f ​ ( 𝝁 ) ) = 𝒢 ⁡ ( f ) \range(H_{f}(\boldsymbol{\mu}))=\mathcal{G}(f) , we have P ⪯ σ − 1 ​ H f ​ ( 𝝁 ) P\preceq\sigma^{-1}H_{f}(\boldsymbol{\mu}) where σ = λ min ​ ( H f ​ ( 𝝁 ) ) \sigma=\lambda_{\min}(H_{f}(\boldsymbol{\mu})) is the smallest positive eigenvalue of H f ​ ( 𝝁 ) H_{f}(\boldsymbol{\mu}) . Thus, we have H f ​ ( 𝝁 ′ ) ≃ ( 1 ± L ​ σ − 1 ​ ∥ 𝝁 ′ − 𝝁 ∥ ) ​ H f ​ ( 𝝁 ) . H_{f}(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime})\simeq\bigl(1\pm L\sigma^{-1}\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\mu}\rVert\bigr)H_{f}(\boldsymbol{\mu})\kern 5.0pt. ∎ A.4 Lipschitz Gradients and Strong Convexity In addition to (or instead of) convexity + , some of our results require Lipschitz gradients or, dually, strong convexity. To be precise, we say that a differentiable function f : ℝ d → ℝ f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R} has a Lipschitz gradient if there exists a constant L L such that ∥ ∇ f ​ ( 𝒖 ) − ∇ f ​ ( 𝒗 ) ∥ ≤ L ⁡ ∥ 𝒖 − 𝒗 ∥ \lVert\nabla f(\boldsymbol{u})-\nabla f(\boldsymbol{v})\rVert\leq L\lVert\boldsymbol{u}-\boldsymbol{v}\rVert for all 𝒖 , 𝒗 ∈ ℝ d \boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d} . If f f is twice differentiable, it suffices to check that ∇ 2 f ​ ( 𝒖 ) ⪯ L ​ I d \nabla^{2}f(\boldsymbol{u})\preceq LI_{d} for all 𝒖 \boldsymbol{u} , where I d ∈ ℝ d × d I_{d}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d} is the identity. We say that f f is strongly convex with the strong convexity constant σ \sigma if f ⁡ ( 𝒗 ) ≥ f ⁡ ( 𝒖 ) + 𝒈 ⊺ ​ ( 𝒗 − 𝒖 ) + 1 2 ​ σ ​ ∥ 𝒗 − 𝒖 ∥ 2 , f(\boldsymbol{v})\geq f(\boldsymbol{u})+\boldsymbol{g}^{\intercal}(\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{u})+\frac{1}{2}\sigma\lVert\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{u}\rVert^{2}\kern 5.0pt, for all 𝒗 , 𝒖 ∈ ℝ d \boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{d} and 𝒈 ∈ ∂ f ⁡ ( 𝒖 ) \boldsymbol{g}\in\partial f(\boldsymbol{u}) . A standard convex analysis result states that if f : ℝ d → ℝ f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R} has a gradient with Lipschitz constant L L then f ∗ f^{*} is strongly convex with the strong convexity constant σ = 1 / L \sigma=1/L (see Prop. 12.60 of Rockafellar and Wets 2009 ). Appendix B Proofs and Additional Results for Section 4 B.1 Proof of Theorem 4.1 We prove a more explicit version of the theorem: Theorem B.1 . Under the exponential trader model, ( 𝐫 ¯ , 𝐜 ¯ , 𝛍 ¯ ) (\bar{\boldsymbol{r}},\bar{\boldsymbol{c}},\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}) is a market-clearing equilibrium if and only if 𝒓 ¯ ∈ arg ⁡ min 𝐫 : ∑ i = 1 N 𝐫 i = 𝟎 ∑ i = 1 N F i ( − 𝐫 i ) , ∑ i = 1 N c ¯ i = 0 , and 𝝁 ¯ = ∇ T ( 𝜽 ~ i − a i 𝐫 ¯ i ) ∀ i ∈ [ N ] . \bar{\boldsymbol{r}}\in\argmin_{\boldsymbol{r}:\&gt;\sum_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{r}_{i}=\mathbf{0}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}F_{i}(-\boldsymbol{r}_{i}),\qquad\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bar{c}_{i}=0,\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=\nabla T(\boldsymbol{\tilde{\theta}}_{i}-a_{i}\bar{\boldsymbol{r}}_{i})\ \ \forall i\in[N]. The conclusions state that both the primal and the dual are attained, and 𝒓 ^ \hat{\boldsymbol{r}} and 𝝁 ^ \hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}} are their solutions if and only if A ⊺ ​ 𝝁 ^ = ∇ f ​ ( − 𝒓 ^ ) A^{\intercal}\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=\nabla f(-\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}) and 𝝁 ^ ∈ ∂ g ⁡ ( A ​ 𝒓 ^ ) \hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\partial g(A\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}) . This establishes that 𝒓 ^ \hat{\boldsymbol{r}} and 𝝁 ^ \hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}} are solutions to the primal ( 15 ) and dual ( 16 ), if and only if they satisfy the conditions in ( 14 ), i.e., if and only if they form a market-clearing equilibrium. This proves the theorem except for the uniqueness of the equilibrium prices 𝝁 ¯ \bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}} . The uniqueness follows from the fact that 𝝁 ¯ \bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}} minimizes ∑ i F i ∗ ​ ( 𝝁 ) \sum_{i}F_{i}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mu}) , and the functions F i ∗ F_{i}^{*} are strongly convex on their domain ℳ \mathcal{M} , which in turn follows because the functions F i F_{i} have Lipschitz gradients (a property they inherit from the log partition function T T ). ∎ B.2 Proof of Theorem 4.2 We prove a more explicit version of the theorem: Theorem B.2 . Under the exponential trader model, ( 𝐫 ⋆ , 𝐜 ⋆ , 𝛍 ⋆ ) (\boldsymbol{r}^{\star},\boldsymbol{c}^{\star},\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\star}) is a market-maker equilibrium for cost function C b C_{b} if and only if, for the market state 𝐬 ⋆ = ∑ i = 1 N 𝐫 i ⋆ \boldsymbol{s}^{\star}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{r}^{\star}_{i} , 𝒓 ⋆ ∈ arg ⁡ min 𝐫 F ( 𝐫 ) , ∑ i = 1 N c i ⋆ = C b ( 𝟎 ) − C b ( 𝐬 ⋆ ) , and 𝝁 ⋆ = ∇ C b ( 𝐬 ⋆ ) = ∇ T ( 𝜽 ~ i − a i 𝐫 i ⋆ ) ∀ i ∈ [ N ] . \boldsymbol{r}^{\star}\in\argmin_{\boldsymbol{r}}F(\boldsymbol{r}),\quad\sum_{i=1}^{N}c^{\star}_{i}=C_{b}(\mathbf{0})-C_{b}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\star}),\quad\text{and}\quad\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\star}=\nabla C_{b}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\star})=\nabla T(\boldsymbol{\tilde{\theta}}_{i}-a_{i}\boldsymbol{r}^{\star}_{i})\ \forall i\in[N]. A market-maker equilibrium always exists. Furthermore, for any market-maker equilibrium, the equilibrium prices are unique solutions of the following dual problem: 𝝁 ⋆ = arg ⁡ min 𝝁 ∈ ℝ K ⁡ [ ∑ i F i ∗ ​ ( 𝝁 ) + bC ∗ ​ ( 𝝁 ) ] . \boldsymbol{\mu}^{\star}=\argmin_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}}\Bigl[\sum_{i}F_{i}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mu})+bC^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mu})\Bigr]\kern 5.0pt. Proof. We proceed similarly to the proof of Theorem B.1 . The conclusions state that both the primal and the dual are attained, and 𝒓 ^ \hat{\boldsymbol{r}} and 𝝁 ^ \hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}} are their solutions if and only if A ⊺ ​ 𝝁 ^ = ∇ f ​ ( − 𝒓 ^ ) A^{\intercal}\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=\nabla f(-\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}) and 𝝁 ^ = ∇ g ​ ( A ​ 𝒓 ^ ) \hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=\nabla g(A\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}) . It remains to show that 𝝁 ⋆ \boldsymbol{\mu}^{\star} is unique. As before this follows by strong convexity of F i ∗ F_{i}^{*} and the fact that 𝝁 ⋆ \boldsymbol{\mu}^{\star} minimizes ∑ i F i ∗ ​ ( 𝝁 ) + b ​ C ∗ ​ ( 𝝁 ) \sum_{i}F_{i}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mu})+bC^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mu}) . ∎ B.3 Proof of Theorem 4.3 By Theorem 4.1 , 𝝁 ¯ = arg ⁡ min 𝝁 ∈ ℝ K ⁡ [ ∑ i F i ∗ ​ ( 𝝁 ) ] \bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=\argmin_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}}[\sum_{i}F_{i}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mu})] and from the first-order optimality 𝟎 ∈ ∂ [ ∑ i F i ∗ ​ ( 𝝁 ¯ ) ] . \mathbf{0}\in\partial\Bigl[\sum_{i}F_{i}^{*}(\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\Bigr]\kern 5.0pt. B.4 Sampling Error The market’s forecasting ability is fundamentally limited by the information present among the population of traders, and the traders’ risk attitudes in communicating their information via trades. In this appendix, we quantify these sources of error by analyzing the discrepancy ∥ 𝝁 true − 𝝁 ¯ ∥ \lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\textup{true}}-\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\rVert between the true expected security values and the market-clearing equilibrium prices. The characterization of 𝝁 ¯ \bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}} in Theorem 4.3 reflects two possible sources of error. First, the beliefs 𝜽 ~ i \boldsymbol{\tilde{\theta}}_{i} are typically noisy signals of the ground truth. Second, beliefs are weighted according to risk aversions a i a_{i} , which can skew the prices. To formalize the latter concept, we write N eff = ( ∑ i a i − 1 ) 2 ( ∑ i a i − 2 ) N_{\mathrm{eff}}=\frac{\left(\sum_{i}a_{i}^{-1}\right)^{2}}{\left(\sum_{i}a_{i}^{-2}\right)} to denote the effective sample size of the weighted average. When risk aversion coefficients are equal across agents, we have N eff = N N_{\mathrm{eff}}=N , and when one agent has much smaller risk aversion than the others, N eff → 1 N_{\mathrm{eff}}\rightarrow 1 . As the next result shows, the magnitude of the sampling error depends on the effective sample size as it relates to the number of securities and the variance in trader beliefs. Theorem B.3 . Under the exponential trader model, assume that the beliefs 𝛉 ~ i \boldsymbol{\tilde{\theta}}_{i} are drawn independently for each trader i ∈ [ N ] i\in[N] with mean 𝔼 ⁡ [ 𝛉 ~ i ] = 𝛉 true \mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{\tilde{\theta}}_{i}]=\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\textup{true}} and covariance 𝕍 ⁡ ( 𝛉 ~ i ) ⪯ σ 2 ​ I K \mathbb{V}(\boldsymbol{\tilde{\theta}}_{i})\preceq\sigma^{2}I_{K} for some σ 2 ≥ 0 \sigma^{2}\geq 0 . For any δ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \delta\in(0,1) , the market-clearing prices 𝛍 ¯ \bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}} satisfy, with probability at least 1 − δ 1-\delta , ∥ 𝛍 ¯ − 𝛍 true ∥ ≤ O ⁡ ( σ ​ K / ( N eff ​ δ ) ) . \lVert\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\textup{true}}\rVert\leq O\left(\,\sigma\sqrt{K/(N_{\mathrm{eff}}\,\delta)}\,\right). Furthermore, assuming that each a i a_{i} lies in a bounded range [ a min , a max ] [a_{\min},a_{\max}] where a min , a max &gt; 0 a_{\min},a_{\max}&gt;0 , we have that N eff → ∞ N_{\mathrm{eff}}\rightarrow\infty as N → ∞ N\rightarrow\infty . Proof. We write w i = a i − 1 / ( ∑ j a j − 1 ) w_{i}=a_{i}^{-1}/(\sum_{j}a_{j}^{-1}) for the weights in the average. Note that N eff = ( ∑ i w i 2 ) − 1 N_{\mathrm{eff}}=(\sum_{i}w_{i}^{2})^{-1} . By the fact that beliefs are independent, we have: 𝔼 ⁡ [ ∑ i = 1 N w i ​ 𝜽 ~ i ] = 𝜽 true ​ and ​ 𝕍 ​ ( ∑ i = 1 N w i ​ 𝜽 ~ i ) ⪯ N eff − 1 ​ σ 2 ​ I K . \mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N}w_{i}\boldsymbol{\tilde{\theta}}_{i}\right]=\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\textup{true}}\kern 5.0pt\mbox{and}\kern 5.0pt\mathbb{V}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}w_{i}\boldsymbol{\tilde{\theta}}_{i}\right)\preceq N_{\mathrm{eff}}^{-1}\sigma^{2}I_{K}. By applying the multidimensional version of Chebyshev’s inequality, we therefore have Pr ⁡ ( ‖ ∑ i = 1 N w i ​ 𝜽 ~ i − 𝜽 true ‖ &gt; t ) ≤ K ​ σ 2 N eff ​ t 2 . \mathrm{Pr}\left(\,\left\|\,\sum_{i=1}^{N}w_{i}\boldsymbol{\tilde{\theta}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\textup{true}}\,\right\|&gt;t\,\right)\leq\frac{K\sigma^{2}}{N_{\mathrm{eff}}\,t^{2}}. The result then follows from the fact that 𝝁 ¯ = ∇ T ​ ( ∑ i w i ​ 𝜽 ~ i ) \bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=\nabla T(\sum_{i}w_{i}\boldsymbol{\tilde{\theta}}_{i}) by Theorem 4.3 , the fact that 𝝁 true = ∇ T ​ ( 𝜽 true ) \boldsymbol{\mu}^{\textup{true}}=\nabla T(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\textup{true}}) , and the Lipschitz continuity of ∇ T \nabla T . For the final claim, we have N eff = ( ∑ i a i − 1 ) 2 ( ∑ i a i − 2 ) ≥ ( ∑ i a max − 1 ) 2 ( ∑ i a min − 2 ) = N ​ ( a min / a max ) 2 . N_{\mathrm{eff}}=\frac{\left(\sum_{i}a_{i}^{-1}\right)^{2}}{\left(\sum_{i}a_{i}^{-2}\right)}\geq\frac{\left(\sum_{i}a_{\max}^{-1}\right)^{2}}{\left(\sum_{i}a_{\min}^{-2}\right)}=N(a_{\min}/a_{\max})^{2}. ∎ Theorem B.3 implies that as the number of traders grows large, the market prices 𝝁 ¯ \bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}} converge to the ground truth 𝝁 true \boldsymbol{\mu}^{\textup{true}} in probability. It is important to note that this relies on Theorem 4.3 , which is an artifact of exponential utility—for general utilities, market-clearing prices may not be consistent in the statistical sense, and this extra discrepancy would need to be quantified in the error decomposition. For a finite number of traders, the bound in Theorem B.3 increases with the belief variance and the number of securities, as one would expect. It decreases with the effective sample size: the information incorporated into market-clearing prices improves when risk aversions are more uniform, and when the number of agents increases. Appendix C Proofs and Additional Results for Section 5 C.1 Proof of Theorem 5.1 Since gradients ∇ F i \nabla F_{i} are Lipschitz, the functions F i ∗ F_{i}^{*} are strongly convex over their domain, which is ℳ \mathcal{M} . Therefore, their sum G ⁡ ( 𝝁 ) ≔ ∑ i F i ∗ ​ ( 𝝁 ) G(\boldsymbol{\mu})\coloneqq\sum_{i}F_{i}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mu}) is also strongly convex on ℳ \mathcal{M} with some strong convexity constant σ \sigma . This together with strong convexity yields the lower bound ∑ i F i ∗ ​ ( 𝝁 ) ≥ ∑ i F i ∗ ​ ( 𝝁 ¯ ) + 1 2 ​ σ ​ ∥ 𝝁 − 𝝁 ¯ ∥ 2 \sum_{i}F_{i}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mu})\geq\sum_{i}F_{i}^{*}(\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}})+\frac{1}{2}\sigma\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}-\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\rVert^{2} . At the same time, C ∗ ​ ( 𝝁 ) C^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mu}) is bounded below by a linear function of the form C ∗ ​ ( 𝝁 ¯ ) + 𝒖 ⊺ ​ ( 𝝁 − 𝝁 ¯ ) C^{*}(\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}})+\boldsymbol{u}^{\intercal}(\boldsymbol{\mu}-\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}) , because C ∗ C^{*} is subdifferentiable at 𝝁 ¯ \bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}} since 𝝁 ¯ ∈ ri ⁡ ℳ ⊆ ri ⁡ dom ⁡ C ∗ \bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\ri\mathcal{M}\subseteq\ri\dom C^{*} . Now from the optimality of 𝝁 ⋆ \boldsymbol{\mu}^{\star} and the lower bounds on ∑ i F i ∗ ​ ( 𝝁 ) \sum_{i}F_{i}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mu}) and C ∗ ​ ( 𝝁 ) C^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mu}) , we have ∑ i F i ∗ ​ ( 𝝁 ¯ ) + b ​ C ∗ ​ ( 𝝁 ¯ ) \displaystyle\sum_{i}F_{i}^{*}(\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}})+bC^{*}(\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}) ≥ ∑ i F i ∗ ​ ( 𝝁 ⋆ ) + b ​ C ∗ ​ ( 𝝁 ⋆ ) \displaystyle\geq\sum_{i}F_{i}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\star})+bC^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\star}) ≥ ( ∑ i F i ∗ ​ ( 𝝁 ¯ ) + 1 2 ​ σ ​ ∥ 𝝁 ⋆ − 𝝁 ¯ ∥ 2 ) + b ⁡ ( C ∗ ​ ( 𝝁 ¯ ) + 𝒖 ⊺ ​ ( 𝝁 ⋆ − 𝝁 ¯ ) ) \displaystyle\geq\bigl(\sum_{i}F_{i}^{*}(\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}})+\frac{1}{2}\sigma\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\star}-\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\rVert^{2}\bigr)+b\bigl(C^{*}(\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}})+\boldsymbol{u}^{\intercal}(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\star}-\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\bigr) ⟹ ∥ 𝝁 ⋆ − 𝝁 ¯ ∥ 2 ≤ − 2 ​ b σ ​ 𝒖 ⊺ ​ ( 𝝁 ⋆ − 𝝁 ¯ ) \displaystyle\implies\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\star}-\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\rVert^{2}\leq-\frac{2b}{\sigma}\boldsymbol{u}^{\intercal}(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\star}-\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}) ⟹ ∥ 𝝁 ⋆ − 𝝁 ¯ ∥ ≤ 2 ​ b σ ​ ∥ 𝒖 ∥ . \displaystyle\implies\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\star}-\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\rVert\leq\frac{2b}{\sigma}\lVert\boldsymbol{u}\rVert\kern 5.0pt. C.2 A Remark on Partial and Incoherent Beliefs The proof of Theorem 5.1 crucially relies on the fact that dom ⁡ ( ∑ i F i ∗ ) = ℳ ⊆ dom ⁡ C ∗ \dom(\sum_{i}F_{i}^{*})=\mathcal{M}\subseteq\dom C^{*} , i.e., that the cost function C C does not force any additional constraints on 𝝁 \boldsymbol{\mu} beyond those already represented by the trader potentials F i F_{i} . This is natural in our setting, because trader utilities restrict the equilibrium prices to lie in the smallest set including all coherent price vectors, ℳ \mathcal{M} . This property of the trader utilities means that the traders would be always willing to trade if the prices were outside the set ℳ \mathcal{M} . If the trader utilities did not have this property, for instance, if each trader was interested in only a few securities, or their beliefs were incoherent, then this result might not hold. In such a setting, we might end up with 𝝁 ¯ ∉ dom ⁡ C ∗ \bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\not\in\dom C^{*} . At best, we could then show that lim b → 0 𝝁 ⋆ ​ ( b , C ) = arg ⁡ min 𝝁 ∈ dom ⁡ C ∗ ⁡ [ ∑ i = 1 N F i ∗ ​ ( 𝝁 ) ] . \lim_{b\to 0}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\star}(b;C)=\myargmin_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\dom C^{*}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N}F^{*}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mu})\right]. This, of course, agrees with Theorem 5.1 for our specific setting when the restriction to dom ⁡ C ∗ \dom C^{*} creates no additional constraints, because dom ⁡ ( ∑ i F i ∗ ) ⊆ dom ⁡ C ∗ \dom(\sum_{i}F_{i}^{*})\subseteq\dom C^{*} . C.3 Proof of Theorem 5.6 The proof will proceed by analyzing the Taylor expansion of the dual objective characterizing 𝝁 ⋆ \boldsymbol{\mu}^{\star} . However, the functions F i ∗ F_{i}^{*} and C ∗ C^{*} might not be differentiable in the standard sense, because their domains might have empty interiors (and only non-empty relative interiors). Fortunately, F i F_{i} and C C are convex + , so by Proposition A.9 there exist convex + functions G i G_{i} and R R that coincide with F i ∗ F^{*}_{i} and C ∗ C^{*} on aff ⁡ dom ⁡ F i ∗ \aff\dom F^{*}_{i} and aff ⁡ dom ⁡ C ∗ \aff\dom C^{*} . These functions are three times continuously differentiable, which is what we need to obtain the third order Taylor expansion. Note that dom ⁡ F i ∗ = ℳ \dom F^{*}_{i}=\mathcal{M} for all i ∈ [ N ] i\in[N] . Let 𝒜 \mathcal{A} denote the affine hull of ℳ = dom ⁡ F i ∗ \mathcal{M}=\dom F^{*}_{i} . Since ℳ ⊆ dom ⁡ C ∗ \mathcal{M}\subseteq\dom C^{*} , the dual in Eq. ( 6 ) is equivalent to 𝝁 ⋆ = arg ⁡ min 𝝁 ∈ 𝒜 ⁡ [ ∑ i = 1 N G i ​ ( 𝝁 ) + bR ⁡ ( 𝝁 ) ] . \boldsymbol{\mu}^{\star}=\myargmin_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{A}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N}G_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mu})+bR(\boldsymbol{\mu})\right]\kern 5.0pt. (20) Note that 𝝁 ¯ ∈ ri ⁡ ℳ \bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\ri\mathcal{M} , because by the definition 𝝁 ¯ = ∇ T ​ ( 𝒔 ¯ ) \bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=\nabla T(\bar{\boldsymbol{s}}) for some 𝒔 ¯ \bar{\boldsymbol{s}} and the gradients of T T are in ri ⁡ ℳ \ri\mathcal{M} . Thus, functions G i G_{i} and R R are differentiable and have Hessians at 𝝁 ¯ \bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}} (by convexity + ). We apply the Taylor expansion at 𝝁 ¯ \bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}} to analyze the value of the objective at 𝝁 ⋆ \boldsymbol{\mu}^{\star} . Let G ⁡ ( 𝝁 ) ≔ ∑ i G i ​ ( 𝝁 ) G(\boldsymbol{\mu})\coloneqq\sum_{i}G_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mu}) . By Mean Value Theorem, we have ∇ G ​ ( 𝝁 ⋆ ) \displaystyle\nabla G(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\star}) = ∇ G ​ ( 𝝁 ¯ ) + ∇ 2 G ​ ( 𝝁 G ) ​ ( 𝝁 ¯ − 𝝁 ⋆ ) \displaystyle=\nabla G(\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}})+\nabla^{2}G(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{G})(\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\star}) ∇ R ​ ( 𝝁 ⋆ ) \displaystyle\nabla R(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\star}) = ∇ R ​ ( 𝝁 ¯ ) + ∇ 2 R ​ ( 𝝁 R ) ​ ( 𝝁 ¯ − 𝝁 ⋆ ) \displaystyle=\nabla R(\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}})+\nabla^{2}R(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{R})(\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\star}) for some 𝝁 G \boldsymbol{\mu}_{G} and 𝝁 R \boldsymbol{\mu}_{R} on the line segment connecting 𝝁 ⋆ \boldsymbol{\mu}^{\star} with 𝝁 ¯ \bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}} . By Theorem 5.1 , ∥ 𝝁 ⋆ − 𝝁 ¯ ∥ = O ⁡ ( b ) \lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\star}-\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\rVert=O(b) as b → 0 b\to 0 , and thus also ∥ 𝝁 G − 𝝁 ¯ ∥ = O ⁡ ( b ) \lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}_{G}-\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\rVert=O(b) and ∥ 𝝁 R − 𝝁 ¯ ∥ = O ⁡ ( b ) \lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}_{R}-\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\rVert=O(b) . By the continuity of third derivatives of G G and R R in the neighborhood of 𝝁 ¯ \bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}} , the Hessians of G G and R R are Lipschitz in some neighborhood of 𝝁 ¯ \bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}} , which means that ∇ 2 G ​ ( 𝝁 G ) \displaystyle\nabla^{2}G(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{G}) = ∇ 2 G ​ ( 𝝁 ¯ ) + Δ G \displaystyle=\nabla^{2}G(\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}})+\Delta_{G} ∇ 2 R ​ ( 𝝁 R ) \displaystyle\nabla^{2}R(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{R}) = ∇ 2 R ​ ( 𝝁 ¯ ) + Δ R \displaystyle=\nabla^{2}R(\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}})+\Delta_{R} where Δ G \Delta_{G} and Δ R \Delta_{R} are matrices with ∥ Δ G ∥ = O ⁡ ( ∥ 𝝁 G − 𝝁 ¯ ∥ ) = O ⁡ ( b ) \lVert\Delta_{G}\rVert=O(\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}_{G}-\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\rVert)=O(b) and ∥ Δ R ∥ = O ⁡ ( ∥ 𝝁 R − 𝝁 ¯ ∥ ) = O ⁡ ( b ) \lVert\Delta_{R}\rVert=O(\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}_{R}-\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\rVert)=O(b) . From the properties of conjugates, 𝒔 ∈ ∂ C ∗ ​ ( 𝝁 ¯ ) \boldsymbol{s}\in\partial C^{*}(\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}) iff ∇ C ​ ( 𝒔 ) = 𝝁 ¯ \nabla C(\boldsymbol{s})=\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}} , so we can obtain 𝒔 LMSR \boldsymbol{s}^{\texttt{{LMSR}}} and 𝒔 IND \boldsymbol{s}^{\texttt{{IND}}} by inverting the gradients of LMSR and IND : s k LMSR = log μ ¯ k , s k IND = log ( μ ¯ k 1 − μ ¯ k ) for all k ∈ [ K ] . \textstyle s^{\texttt{{LMSR}}}_{k}=\log\bar{\mu}_{k}\kern 5.0pt,\quad s^{\texttt{{IND}}}_{k}=\log\left(\frac{\bar{\mu}_{k}}{1-\bar{\mu}_{k}}\right)\quad\text{for all $k\in[K]$.} Note that both s k LMSR s^{\texttt{{LMSR}}}_{k} and s k IND s^{\texttt{{IND}}}_{k} are monotone transformations of μ ¯ k \bar{\mu}_{k} , and since 𝝁 ¯ \bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}} is sorted, so must be 𝒔 LMSR \boldsymbol{s}^{\texttt{{LMSR}}} and 𝒔 IND \boldsymbol{s}^{\texttt{{IND}}} . For any k ∈ [ K − 1 ] k\in[K-1] , we have s k LMSR − s k + 1 LMSR = log ⁡ ( μ ¯ k μ ¯ k + 1 ) s^{\texttt{{LMSR}}}_{k}-s^{\texttt{{LMSR}}}_{k+1}=\log\left(\frac{\bar{\mu}_{k}}{\bar{\mu}_{k+1}}\right) and we also have s k IND − s k + 1 IND \displaystyle s^{\texttt{{IND}}}_{k}-s^{\texttt{{IND}}}_{k+1} = log ⁡ ( μ ¯ k μ ¯ k + 1 ⋅ 1 − μ ¯ k + 1 1 − μ ¯ k ) \displaystyle=\log\left(\frac{\bar{\mu}_{k}}{\bar{\mu}_{k+1}}\cdot\frac{1-\bar{\mu}_{k+1}}{1-\bar{\mu}_{k}}\right) = log ⁡ ( μ ¯ k μ ¯ k + 1 ⋅ c k + μ ¯ k c k + μ ¯ k + 1 ) = log ⁡ ( μ ¯ k μ ¯ k + 1 ) + log ⁡ ( c k + μ ¯ k c k + μ ¯ k + 1 ) , \displaystyle=\log\left(\frac{\bar{\mu}_{k}}{\bar{\mu}_{k+1}}\cdot\frac{c_{k}+\bar{\mu}_{k}}{c_{k}+\bar{\mu}_{k+1}}\right)=\log\left(\frac{\bar{\mu}_{k}}{\bar{\mu}_{k+1}}\right)+\log\left(\frac{c_{k}+\bar{\mu}_{k}}{c_{k}+\bar{\mu}_{k+1}}\right)\kern 5.0pt, where c k ≔ 1 − μ ¯ k − μ ¯ k + 1 ≥ 0 c_{k}\coloneqq 1-\bar{\mu}_{k}-\bar{\mu}_{k+1}\geq 0 . We refer to such a model as trader dynamics . We consider two simple trader dynamics: • All-securities dynamics ( ASD ). In each round, a trader i ∈ [ N ] i\in[N] is chosen uniformly at random. This trader then buys a bundle 𝜹 ∈ ℝ K \boldsymbol{\delta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K} which optimizes her utility, i.e., if the current state of the market is 𝒔 \boldsymbol{s} and the current cash and security allocations of the trader are c i c_{i} and 𝒓 i \boldsymbol{r}_{i} , then the trader picks 𝜹 \boldsymbol{\delta} maximizing U i ​ ( 𝒓 i + 𝜹 , c i − C b ​ ( 𝒔 + 𝜹 ) + C b ​ ( 𝒔 ) ) U_{i}(\boldsymbol{r}_{i}+\boldsymbol{\delta},\,c_{i}-C_{b}(\boldsymbol{s}+\boldsymbol{\delta})+C_{b}(\boldsymbol{s})) . • Single-security dynamics ( SSD ). In each round, a trader i ∈ [ N ] i\in[N] is chosen uniformly at random and this trader picks a security k ∈ [ K ] k\in[K] uniformly at random. The trader then buys a quantity δ ∈ ℝ \delta\in\mathbb{R} of the k k th security to optimize her utility. Let 𝒆 k \boldsymbol{e}_{k} be the k k th vector of the standard basis. Then the trader picks δ \delta maximizing U i ​ ( 𝒓 i + δ ​ 𝒆 k , c i − C b ​ ( 𝒔 + δ ​ 𝒆 k ) + C b ​ ( 𝒔 ) ) U_{i}(\boldsymbol{r}_{i}+\delta\boldsymbol{e}_{k},\,c_{i}-C_{b}(\boldsymbol{s}+\delta\boldsymbol{e}_{k})+C_{b}(\boldsymbol{s})) . The all-securities dynamics has been recently studied by Frongillo and Reid 2015 . This model assumes that traders are able to calculate the optimal bundle over all securities, which may not be a realistic assumption when the number of securities is large. The single-security dynamics, in which we only assume that traders can optimize over a single security at a time, is more appropriate for computationally limited traders. We formalize both dynamics in a unified analysis via blocks . Specifically, we assume that the coordinates [ N ] × [ K ] [N]\times[K] are partitioned into blocks α ∈ 𝒜 \alpha\in\mathcal{A} , where α ⊆ [ N ] × [ K ] \alpha\subseteq[N]\times[K] , such that ⨄ α ∈ 𝒜 α = [ N ] × [ K ] \biguplus_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\alpha=[N]\times[K] . Blocks are disjoint subsets of coordinates of the overall allocation vector 𝒓 ∈ ℝ N ​ K \boldsymbol{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{NK} . We further assume that each block α ∈ 𝒜 \alpha\in\mathcal{A} is fully contained within coordinates corresponding to some trader i i . Thus each block α \alpha can be written as { i } × β \{i\}\times\beta for some i ∈ [ N ] i\in[N] and β ⊆ [ K ] \beta\subseteq[K] . For ASD , we have N N blocks 𝒜 = { { i } × [ K ] : i ∈ [ N ] } \mathcal{A}=\bigl\{\{i\}\times[K]:\&gt;i\in[N]\bigr\} . For SSD , we have N ​ K NK blocks 𝒜 = { { i } × { k } : i ∈ [ N ] , k ∈ [ K ] } \mathcal{A}=\bigl\{\{i\}\times\{k\}:\&gt;i\in[N],k\in[K]\bigr\} . Let E α ∈ ℝ N ​ K × | α | E_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}^{NK\times\lvert\alpha\rvert} be the embedding matrix for the block α \alpha . It maps | α | \lvert\alpha\rvert -dimensional vectors 𝒖 ∈ ℝ | α | \boldsymbol{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{\lvert\alpha\rvert} to vectors 𝒗 ∈ ℝ N ​ K \boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{NK} that are zero everywhere except for the block α \alpha , where they coincide with 𝒖 \boldsymbol{u} . The range of E α E_{\alpha} is exactly the set of vectors that are zero outside the block α \alpha . Its transpose E α ⊺ E_{\alpha}^{\intercal} projects vectors from ℝ N ​ K \mathbb{R}^{NK} to ℝ | α | \mathbb{R}^{\lvert\alpha\rvert} by removing all coordinates outside the block α \alpha . For any subset β ⊆ [ K ] \beta\subseteq[K] , we similarly define the embedding matrices E β ∈ ℝ K × | β | E_{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times\lvert\beta\rvert} . The next theorem shows that optimizing utility, as in ASD or SSD , corresponds to the greedy optimization of the potential function F F along the coordinates of the corresponding block: Theorem D.1 . Let α = { i } × β \alpha=\{i\}\times\beta , where β ⊆ [ K ] \beta\subseteq[K] , be a block of coordinates controlled by trader i i . Assume that trader i i has security bundle 𝐫 i \boldsymbol{r}_{i} and c i c_{i} units of cash, and the current market state is 𝐬 \boldsymbol{s} . Then arg ⁡ max 𝜹 ∈ range ⁡ ( E β ) ⁡ U i ​ ( 𝐫 i + 𝜹 , c i − C b ​ ( 𝐬 + 𝜹 ) + C b ​ ( 𝐬 ) ) = arg ⁡ min 𝜹 ∈ range ⁡ ( E β ) ⁡ F ​ ( 𝐫 − i , 𝐫 i + 𝜹 ) , \argmax_{\boldsymbol{\delta}\in\range(E_{\beta})}U_{i}(\boldsymbol{r}_{i}+\boldsymbol{\delta},\,c_{i}-C_{b}(\boldsymbol{s}+\boldsymbol{\delta})+C_{b}(\boldsymbol{s}))=\argmin_{\boldsymbol{\delta}\in\range(E_{\beta})}F(\boldsymbol{r}_{-i},\,\boldsymbol{r}_{i}+\boldsymbol{\delta})\kern 5.0pt, where 𝐫 − i \boldsymbol{r}_{-i} denotes the concatenation of 𝐫 j \boldsymbol{r}_{j} across j ≠ i j\neq i . Proof. The proof is immediate from the definition of the potential F F . ∎ D.2 Relationship between the Suboptimality of Potential and the Convergence Error In this appendix, we relate the suboptimality of the objective to several other quantities used in analysis of convergence error. We begin by defining these quantities. Given an allocation vector 𝒓 ∈ R N ​ K \boldsymbol{r}\in R^{NK} , the associated market price (the gradient of the cost) will be denoted 𝝁 0 ​ ( 𝒓 ) \boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}(\boldsymbol{r}) and the gradients of trader potentials will be denoted 𝝁 i ​ ( 𝒓 ) \boldsymbol{\mu}_{i}(\boldsymbol{r}) : 𝝁 0 ​ ( 𝒓 ) \displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}(\boldsymbol{r}) ≔ ∇ C b ​ ( ∑ i = 1 N 𝒓 i ) = ∇ C ​ ( ∑ i = 1 N 𝒓 i / b ) \displaystyle\coloneqq\nabla C_{b}\bigl(\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{r}_{i}\bigr)=\nabla C\bigl(\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{r}_{i}/b\bigr) 𝝁 i ​ ( 𝒓 ) \displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}_{i}(\boldsymbol{r}) ≔ ∇ F i ( − 𝒓 i ) = ∇ T ( 𝜽 ~ i − a i 𝒓 i ) for i ∈ [ N ] \displaystyle\coloneqq\nabla F_{i}(-\boldsymbol{r}_{i})=\nabla T(\boldsymbol{\tilde{\theta}}_{i}-a_{i}\boldsymbol{r}_{i})\qquad\text{for $i\in[N]$} where T T is the log partition function. As in the body of the paper, let 𝒓 ⋆ \boldsymbol{r}^{\star} denote an arbitrary minimizer of F F and let F ⋆ F^{\star} denote the minimum value of F F . From Theorem B.2 , at any equilibrium allocation 𝒓 ⋆ \boldsymbol{r}^{\star} , 𝝁 0 ​ ( 𝒓 ⋆ ) = 𝝁 ⋆ and 𝝁 i ​ ( 𝒓 ⋆ ) = 𝝁 ⋆ ​ for all i ∈ [ N ] . \boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}(\boldsymbol{r}^{\star})=\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\star}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\boldsymbol{\mu}_{i}(\boldsymbol{r}^{\star})=\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\star}\text{ for all $i\in[N]$.} Let 𝒓 t \boldsymbol{r}^{t} denote the allocation vector after the t t th trade, and 𝝁 t ≔ 𝝁 0 ​ ( 𝒓 t ) \boldsymbol{\mu}^{t}\coloneqq\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}(\boldsymbol{r}^{t}) be the corresponding market price. We next show that to bound the convergence error ∥ 𝝁 0 ​ ( 𝒓 t ) − 𝝁 ⋆ ∥ \lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}(\boldsymbol{r}^{t})-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\star}\rVert it suffices to bound the suboptimality of the current objective value, F ⁡ ( 𝒓 t ) − F ⋆ F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t})-F^{\star} . In fact, we show that the suboptimality F ⁡ ( 𝒓 t ) − F ⋆ F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t})-F^{\star} simultaneously also bounds ∥ 𝝁 i ​ ( 𝒓 t ) − 𝝁 ⋆ ∥ \lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}_{i}(\boldsymbol{r}^{t})-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\star}\rVert , which can be viewed as a measure of suboptimality of individual traders and will be used in our later analysis. We first prove this result when C C has a Lipschitz-continuous gradient and then for the case when C C is convex + . Theorem D.2 . The key difference from the standard analysis of Nesterov [ 11 ] is the focus on the local convergence in the neighborhood of the solution, rather than global convergence. This analysis is not specific to our setting, and may be of independent interest. We consider the optimization problem min 𝒓 ∈ ℝ N ​ K ⁡ F ⁡ ( 𝒓 ) , \min_{\boldsymbol{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{NK}}F(\boldsymbol{r})\kern 5.0pt, where F : ℝ N ​ K → ℝ F:\mathbb{R}^{NK}\to\mathbb{R} is a differentiable convex function bounded below. We are given a set of blocks α ∈ 𝒜 \alpha\in\mathcal{A} , which partition the coordinates [ N ] × [ K ] [N]\times[K] . The block-coordinate descent algorithm sets the initial iterate 𝒓 0 = 𝟎 \boldsymbol{r}^{0}=\mathbf{0} , and in each iteration chooses an index α ∈ 𝒜 \alpha\in\mathcal{A} uniformly at random and fully optimizes the objective over the coordinates in block α \alpha : given the current iterate 𝒓 t \boldsymbol{r}^{t} , the new iterate is 𝒓 t + 1 = Ψ α ​ ( 𝒓 t ) \boldsymbol{r}^{t+1}=\Psi_{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{r}^{t}) where Ψ α ​ ( 𝒓 ) ≔ arg ⁡ min 𝐫 ′ ∈ 𝐫 + range ⁡ ( E α ) ⁡ F ​ ( 𝐫 ′ ) \Psi_{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{r})\coloneqq\myargmin_{\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}\in\boldsymbol{r}+\range(E_{\alpha})}F(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}) (48) and E α E_{\alpha} is the embedding matrix for the block α \alpha as introduced in Appendix D.1 . Nesterov 2012 shows that when the optimization objective is strongly convex, it is possible to achieve the linear convergence rate of the form 𝔼 ⁡ [ F ⁡ ( 𝒓 t ) ] − F ⋆ ≤ c ​ γ t \mathbb{E}\left[F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t})\right]-F^{\star}\leq c\gamma^{t} for some constants c &gt; 0 c&gt;0 and γ &lt; 1 \gamma&lt;1 . While the objective in our setting is not globally strongly convex, it is strongly convex locally, so the optimization eventually stays within a region where the strong convexity constant is bounded away from zero, yielding a linear convergence rate. Recall from Section 6 that γ high \gamma_{\textup{high}} is an upper bound on the local convergence rate of an algorithm if the algorithm with probability 1 reaches an iteration t 0 t_{0} such that for some c &gt; 0 c&gt;0 and all t ≥ t 0 t\geq t_{0} , 𝔼 ⁡ [ F ⁡ ( 𝒓 t ) | 𝒓 t 0 ] − F ⋆ ≤ c ​ γ high t − t 0 . \mathbb{E}\left[F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t})\mathbin{\bigm|}\boldsymbol{r}^{t_{0}}\right]-F^{\star}\leq c\gamma_{\textup{high}}^{t-t_{0}}\kern 5.0pt. Also, γ low \gamma_{\textup{low}} is a lower bound on the local convergence rate of an algorithm if γ high ≥ γ low \gamma_{\textup{high}}\geq\gamma_{\textup{low}} holds for all upper bounds γ high \gamma_{\textup{high}} . Our local convergence-rate results are based on various local properties of F F , by which we mean properties that hold on some proper level set of F F , in the sense of the following definition: Definition D.4 (Level Set) . For a given λ ∈ ℝ \lambda\in\mathbb{R} , the level set S ⁡ ( F , λ ) S(F,\lambda) of a function F F is defined as the set of points 𝒓 \boldsymbol{r} where F F is at most λ \lambda : S ⁡ ( F , λ ) ≔ { 𝒓 : F ⁡ ( 𝒓 ) ≤ λ } . S(F,\lambda)\coloneqq\{\boldsymbol{r}:\&gt;F(\boldsymbol{r})\leq\lambda\}\kern 5.0pt. The level set S ⁡ ( F , λ ) S(F,\lambda) is called proper if λ &gt; F ⋆ \lambda&gt;F^{\star} . Our first result requires that the objective be locally strongly convex and smooth in the sense of the following definition: Definition D.5 (Strong Convexity and Smoothness for Matrix Seminorms) . Let A A and B B be symmetric positive-semidefinite matrices. We say that a differentiable function F F is strongly convex on a set S S with respect to A A and smooth on S S with respect to B B if 1 2 𝜹 ⊺ A 𝜹 ≤ F ( 𝒓 + 𝜹 ) − F ( 𝒓 ) − 𝜹 ⊺ ∇ F ( 𝒓 ) ≤ 1 2 𝜹 ⊺ B 𝜹 \frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\delta}^{\intercal}A\boldsymbol{\delta}\leq F(\boldsymbol{r}+\boldsymbol{\delta})-F(\boldsymbol{r})-\boldsymbol{\delta}^{\intercal}\nabla F(\boldsymbol{r})\leq\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\delta}^{\intercal}B\boldsymbol{\delta} whenever 𝒓 ∈ S \boldsymbol{r}\in S and 𝒓 + 𝜹 ∈ S \boldsymbol{r}+\boldsymbol{\delta}\in S . To state the theorem, we introduce additional notation. For a matrix M ∈ ℝ N ​ K × N ​ K M\in\mathbb{R}^{NK\times NK} , let M α , α M_{\alpha,\alpha} denote the block consisting of rows and columns in α \alpha , i.e., M α , α ≔ E α ⊺ ​ M ​ E α M_{\alpha,\alpha}\coloneqq E_{\alpha}^{\intercal}ME_{\alpha} . And let 𝒟 : ℝ N ​ K × N ​ K → ℝ N ​ K × N ​ K \mathcal{D}:\mathbb{R}^{NK\times NK}\to\mathbb{R}^{NK\times NK} be the operation of retaining only the block diagonal of a matrix, i.e., 𝒟 ⁡ ( M ) ≔ diag α ∈ 𝒜 ⁡ M α , α \mathcal{D}(M)\coloneqq\diag_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}M_{\alpha,\alpha} . Recall that M + M^{+} denotes the pseudoinverse of M M . Finally, let 𝒈 t ≔ ∇ F ​ ( 𝒓 t ) \boldsymbol{g}^{t}\coloneqq\nabla F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t}) denote the gradient of the objective in the t t th iteration. Theorem D.6 . Assume that F F attains a minimum and let S ≔ S ⁡ ( F , λ ) S\coloneqq S(F,\lambda) be a proper level set which satisfies the following conditions: 1. F F is strongly convex and smooth on S S with respect to some positive-semidefinite matrices A A and B B such that 𝒢 ⁡ ( F ) ⊆ range ⁡ ( A ) \mathcal{G}(F)\subseteq\range(A) , 𝒢 ⁡ ( F ) ⊆ range ⁡ ( B ) \mathcal{G}(F)\subseteq\range(B) . 2. There exist non-negative constants σ low ≤ σ high ≤ ∞ \sigma_{\textup{low}}\leq\sigma_{\textup{high}}\leq\infty and ℓ &lt; ∞ \ell&lt;\infty such that whenever some iterate 𝒓 t 0 \boldsymbol{r}^{t_{0}} lies in S S , then all the consecutive iterates with t ≥ t 0 + ℓ t\geq t_{0}+\ell satisfy σ low ​ 𝔼 ​ [ ( 𝒈 t ) ⊺ ​ A + ​ 𝒈 t | 𝒓 t 0 ] \displaystyle\sigma_{\textup{low}}\mathbb{E}\left[(\boldsymbol{g}^{t})^{\intercal}A^{+}\boldsymbol{g}^{t}\mathbin{\Bigm|}\boldsymbol{r}^{t_{0}}\right] ≤ 𝔼 ⁡ [ ( 𝒈 t ) ⊺ ​ 𝒟 ​ ( B ) + ​ 𝒈 t | 𝒓 t 0 ] , \displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[(\boldsymbol{g}^{t})^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}(B)^{+}\boldsymbol{g}^{t}\mathbin{\Bigm|}\boldsymbol{r}^{t_{0}}\right]\kern 5.0pt, 𝔼 ⁡ [ ( 𝒈 t ) ⊺ ​ 𝒟 ​ ( A ) + ​ 𝒈 t | 𝒓 t 0 ] \displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[(\boldsymbol{g}^{t})^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}(A)^{+}\boldsymbol{g}^{t}\mathbin{\Bigm|}\boldsymbol{r}^{t_{0}}\right] ≤ σ high ​ 𝔼 ​ [ ( 𝒈 t ) ⊺ ​ B + ​ 𝒈 t | 𝒓 t 0 ] , \displaystyle\leq\sigma_{\textup{high}}\mathbb{E}\left[(\boldsymbol{g}^{t})^{\intercal}B^{+}\boldsymbol{g}^{t}\mathbin{\Bigm|}\boldsymbol{r}^{t_{0}}\right]\kern 5.0pt, where the expectation is over the random choice of updates by the algorithm. Then if F ⋆ &lt; F ⁡ ( 𝐫 t 0 ) &lt; λ F^{\star}&lt;F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t_{0}})&lt;\lambda and t ≥ t 0 + ℓ t\geq t_{0}+\ell , we have max ⁡ { 1 − σ high | 𝒜 | , 0 } ≤ 𝔼 ⁡ [ F ⁡ ( 𝒓 t + 1 ) | 𝒓 t 0 ] − F ⋆ 𝔼 ⁡ [ F ⁡ ( 𝒓 t ) | 𝒓 t 0 ] − F ⋆ ≤ 1 − σ low | 𝒜 | . \max\left\{1-\frac{\sigma_{\textup{high}}}{\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert}\;,\;0\right\}\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t+1})\mathbin{\bigm|}\boldsymbol{r}^{t_{0}}\right]-F^{\star}}{\mathbb{E}\left[F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t})\mathbin{|}\boldsymbol{r}^{t_{0}}\right]-F^{\star}}\leq 1-\frac{\sigma_{\textup{low}}}{\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert}\kern 5.0pt. The proof is deferred to a separate subsection below (Appendix D.4 ). We next show how to apply Theorem D.6 to obtain bounds on local convergence rate. For the lower bounds, we assume that the optimization problem is non-degenerate in the sense that the probability of reaching an iterate 𝒓 t \boldsymbol{r}^{t} which attains a minimum is zero; in other words, only approximate solutions are reached in a finite number of steps. This condition holds for the potential function from the main paper under all-security dynamics ( ASD ), whenever there are at least three agents with distinct beliefs. Proposition D.7 . Let S in = S ⁡ ( F , λ in ) S_{\textup{in}}=S(F,\lambda_{\textup{in}}) and S out = S ⁡ ( F , λ out ) S_{\textup{out}}=S(F,\lambda_{\textup{out}}) be level sets with F ⋆ &lt; λ in &lt; λ out F^{\star}&lt;\lambda_{\textup{in}}&lt;\lambda_{\textup{out}} , and assume that the conditions of Theorem D.6 hold for the level set S out S_{\textup{out}} with matrices A A and B B , and non-negative scalars σ low ≤ σ high ≤ ∞ \sigma_{\textup{low}}\leq\sigma_{\textup{high}}\leq\infty and ℓ &lt; ∞ \ell&lt;\infty . Then for every 𝐫 t 0 ∈ S in \boldsymbol{r}^{t_{0}}\in S_{\textup{in}} there exists c &gt; 0 c&gt;0 such that for all t ≥ t 0 t\geq t_{0} 𝔼 ⁡ [ F ⁡ ( 𝒓 t ) | 𝒓 t 0 ] − F ⋆ ≤ c ​ γ high t − t 0 , \mathbb{E}\left[F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t})\mathbin{|}\boldsymbol{r}^{t_{0}}\right]-F^{\star}\leq c\gamma_{\textup{high}}^{t-t_{0}}\kern 5.0pt, where γ high = 1 − σ low / | 𝒜 | \gamma_{\textup{high}}=1-\sigma_{\textup{low}}/\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert . Furthermore, if the optimization problem is non-degenerate then for every 𝐫 t 0 ∈ S in \boldsymbol{r}^{t_{0}}\in S_{\textup{in}} , which has a non-zero probability of occurring, and all t 1 ≥ t 0 t_{1}\geq t_{0} , there exists c &gt; 0 c&gt;0 such that for all t ≥ t 1 t\geq t_{1} 𝔼 ⁡ [ F ⁡ ( 𝒓 t ) | 𝒓 t 1 ] − F ⋆ ≥ c ​ γ low t − t 1 , \mathbb{E}\left[F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t})\mathbin{|}\boldsymbol{r}^{t_{1}}\right]-F^{\star}\geq c\gamma_{\textup{low}}^{t-t_{1}}\kern 5.0pt, where γ low = max ⁡ { 1 − σ high / | 𝒜 | , 0 } \gamma_{\textup{low}}=\max\{1-\sigma_{\textup{high}}/\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert,\,0\} . Proof. For the upper bound, if F ⁡ ( 𝒓 t 0 ) = F ⋆ F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t_{0}})=F^{\star} , the bound holds for any c &gt; 0 c&gt;0 . Otherwise, set c ≔ γ high − ℓ ​ ( F ⁡ ( 𝒓 t 0 ) − F ⋆ ) c\coloneqq\gamma_{\textup{high}}^{-\ell}\bigl(F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t_{0}})-F^{\star}\bigr) . Since the optimization does not increase the objective, this guarantees that the bound holds for t 0 ≤ t ≤ t 0 + ℓ t_{0}\leq t\leq t_{0}+\ell . For t ≥ t 0 + ℓ t\geq t_{0}+\ell , Theorem D.6 gives 𝔼 ⁡ [ F ⁡ ( 𝒓 t + 1 ) | 𝒓 t 0 ] − F ⋆ ≤ γ high ⋅ ( 𝔼 ⁡ [ F ⁡ ( 𝒓 t ) | 𝒓 t 0 ] − F ⋆ ) , \mathbb{E}\left[F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t+1})\mathbin{|}\boldsymbol{r}^{t_{0}}\right]-F^{\star}\leq\gamma_{\textup{high}}\cdot\bigl(\mathbb{E}\left[F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t})\mathbin{|}\boldsymbol{r}^{t_{0}}\right]-F^{\star}\bigr)\kern 5.0pt, and the upper bound now follows by induction. For the lower bound, note that the non-degeneracy guarantees that F ⁡ ( 𝒓 t ) &gt; F ⋆ F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t})&gt;F^{\star} for all t ≥ t 0 t\geq t_{0} . If γ low = 0 \gamma_{\textup{low}}=0 , the bound holds for any c c . Next consider the case when γ low &gt; 0 \gamma_{\textup{low}}&gt;0 . Pick t 1 ≥ t 0 t_{1}\geq t_{0} and set c ≔ min t 1 ≤ t ≤ t 1 + ℓ ⁡ γ low − ℓ ​ ( 𝔼 ⁡ [ F ⁡ ( 𝒓 t ) | 𝒓 t 1 ] − F ⋆ ) , c\coloneqq\min_{t_{1}\leq t\leq t_{1}+\ell}\;\gamma_{\textup{low}}^{-\ell}\bigl(\mathbb{E}\left[F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t})\mathbin{|}\boldsymbol{r}^{t_{1}}\right]-F^{\star}\bigr)\kern 5.0pt, which is non-zero, because γ low ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] \gamma_{\textup{low}}\in(0,1] and F ⁡ ( 𝒓 t ) &gt; F ⋆ F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t})&gt;F^{\star} . This guarantees that the bound holds up to t = t 1 + ℓ t=t_{1}+\ell . For larger t t , it follows by Theorem D.6 and induction. ∎ When the objective is convex + , we can use the Hessian at the minimum of F F to obtain both a quadratic lower and upper bound on F F , yielding the following bounds on the local convergence rate: Theorem D.8 . Let F F be a convex + function attaining a minimum at 𝐫 ⋆ \boldsymbol{r}^{\star} . Let H ⋆ ≔ ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝐫 ⋆ ) H^{\star}\coloneqq\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{r}^{\star}) and assume there exists λ &gt; F ⋆ \lambda&gt;F^{\star} and non-negative constants σ low \sigma_{\textup{low}} , σ high \sigma_{\textup{high}} , and ℓ \ell such that σ low ≤ 𝔼 ⁡ [ ( 𝒈 t ) ⊺ ​ 𝒟 ​ ( H ⋆ ) + ​ 𝒈 t | 𝒓 t − ℓ ] 𝔼 ⁡ [ ( 𝒈 t ) ⊺ ​ ( H ⋆ ) + ​ 𝒈 t | 𝒓 t − ℓ ] ≤ σ high \sigma_{\textup{low}}\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\;(\boldsymbol{g}^{t})^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}(H^{\star})^{+}\boldsymbol{g}^{t}\;\mathbin{\bigm|}\;\boldsymbol{r}^{t-\ell}\;\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[\;(\boldsymbol{g}^{t})^{\intercal}(H^{\star})^{+}\boldsymbol{g}^{t}\;\mathbin{\bigm|}\;\boldsymbol{r}^{t-\ell}\;\right]}\leq\sigma_{\textup{high}} (49) whenever F ⁡ ( 𝐫 t − ℓ ) ≤ λ F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t-\ell})\leq\lambda , where the expectation is over the choice of updates by the algorithm. Then, for all ε &gt; 0 \varepsilon&gt;0 , the local convergence rate is bounded above by γ high = 1 − σ low / | 𝒜 | + ε \gamma_{\textup{high}}=1-\sigma_{\textup{low}}/\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert+\varepsilon . If the optimization problem is non-degenerate, the local convergence is also bounded below by γ low = 1 − σ high / | 𝒜 | \gamma_{\textup{low}}=1-\sigma_{\textup{high}}/\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert . We defer the proof of Theorem D.8 to a separate subsection (Appendix D.5 ). The ratio bounded in Eq. ( 49 ) can be interpreted as a curvature of the quadratic form 𝒟 ​ ( H ⋆ ) + \mathcal{D}(H^{\star})^{+} under the norm described by the quadratic form ( H ⋆ ) + (H^{\star})^{+} . Larger values of the ratio (larger curvature) mean faster convergence. We will refer to the bounds σ low \sigma_{\textup{low}} and σ high \sigma_{\textup{high}} as lower and upper bounds on local strong convexity of F F (under randomized block-coordinate descent updates). Any non-trivial lower bound, i.e., σ low &gt; 0 \sigma_{\textup{low}}&gt;0 , yields local linear convergence rate since it implies γ high &lt; 1 \gamma_{\textup{high}}&lt;1 , since ε \varepsilon can be chosen arbitrarily small. If we know the Hessian H ⋆ H^{\star} , we can obtain a simple lower bound σ low \sigma_{\textup{low}} and a linear convergence by considering all directions in the span of gradients (and setting ℓ = 0 \ell=0 ). The span of gradients coincides with 𝒢 ⁡ ( F ) \mathcal{G}(F) , because 𝟎 \mathbf{0} is a valid gradient (since F F attains a minimum). Thus, we can obtain σ low \sigma_{\textup{low}} by the following generalized eigenvalue calculation: σ low = min 𝒖 ∈ 𝒢 ⁡ ( F ) \ { 𝟎 } ⁡ 𝒖 ⊺ ​ 𝒟 ​ ( H ⋆ ) + ​ 𝒖 𝒖 ⊺ ​ ( H ⋆ ) + ​ 𝒖 = λ min ​ ( ( H ⋆ ) 1 / 2 ​ 𝒟 ​ ( H ⋆ ) + ​ ( H ⋆ ) 1 / 2 ) , \sigma_{\textup{low}}=\min_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{G}(F)\backslash\{\mathbf{0}\}}\frac{\boldsymbol{u}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}(H^{\star})^{+}\boldsymbol{u}}{\boldsymbol{u}^{\intercal}(H^{\star})^{+}\boldsymbol{u}}=\lambda_{\min}\bigl((H^{\star})^{1/2}\mathcal{D}(H^{\star})^{+}(H^{\star})^{1/2}\bigr)\kern 5.0pt, (50) where λ min ​ ( ⋅ ) \lambda_{\min}(\cdot) is the smallest positive eigenvalue. This is a valid setting of σ low \sigma_{\textup{low}} , because for all 𝒖 ∈ 𝒢 ⁡ ( F ) \boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{G}(F) we then have σ low ​ 𝒖 ⊺ ​ ( H ⋆ ) + ​ 𝒖 ≤ 𝒖 ⊺ ​ 𝒟 ​ ( H ⋆ ) + ​ 𝒖 \sigma_{\textup{low}}\boldsymbol{u}^{\intercal}(H^{\star})^{+}\boldsymbol{u}\leq\boldsymbol{u}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}(H^{\star})^{+}\boldsymbol{u} and therefore, when F ⁡ ( 𝒓 t − ℓ ) ≤ λ F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t-\ell})\leq\lambda , also σ low ​ 𝔼 ​ [ ( 𝒈 t ) ⊺ ​ ( H ⋆ ) + ​ 𝒈 t | 𝒓 t − ℓ ] ≤ 𝔼 ⁡ [ ( 𝒈 t ) ⊺ ​ 𝒟 ​ ( H ⋆ ) + ​ 𝒈 t | 𝒓 t − ℓ ] . \sigma_{\textup{low}}{\mathbb{E}\left[\;(\boldsymbol{g}^{t})^{\intercal}(H^{\star})^{+}\boldsymbol{g}^{t}\;\mathbin{\bigm|}\;\boldsymbol{r}^{t-\ell}\;\right]}\leq{\mathbb{E}\left[\;(\boldsymbol{g}^{t})^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}(H^{\star})^{+}\boldsymbol{g}^{t}\;\mathbin{\bigm|}\;\boldsymbol{r}^{t-\ell}\;\right]}. This value of σ low \sigma_{\textup{low}} is non-zero, because 𝒢 ⁡ ( F ) = range ⁡ ( H ⋆ ) + ⊆ range ⁡ 𝒟 ​ ( H ⋆ ) + \mathcal{G}(F)=\range(H^{\star})^{+}\subseteq\range\mathcal{D}(H^{\star})^{+} . We pursue this style of analysis for single-securities dynamics ( SSD ), where we derive σ low \sigma_{\textup{low}} using Eq. ( 50 ), but do so directly in terms of Hessians of functions C C and T T rather than the potential F F . For all-securities dynamics, we consider ℓ &gt; 0 \ell&gt;0 and take advantage of the averaging effect of expectations in Eq. ( 49 ), which yields a tighter lower bound σ low \sigma_{\textup{low}} and a non-trivial upper bound σ high \sigma_{\textup{high}} . D.4 Proof of Theorem D.6 Proof of Theorem D.6 . The vector of partial derivatives of F F within block α \alpha will be called partial gradient and denoted ∇ α F ( 𝒓 ) ≔ E α ⊺ ∇ F ( 𝒓 ) \nabla_{\alpha}F(\boldsymbol{r})\coloneqq E_{\alpha}^{\intercal}\nabla F(\boldsymbol{r}) . Since F F has a minimizer, its set of gradients contains a zero, and therefore its gradient space 𝒢 ⁡ ( F ) \mathcal{G}(F) coincides with the span of its gradients (see Definition A.5 ). We also define the set 𝒢 α ​ ( F ) ≔ E α ⊺ ​ 𝒢 ​ ( F ) \mathcal{G}_{\alpha}(F)\coloneqq E_{\alpha}^{\intercal}\mathcal{G}(F) , which then coincides with the span of partial gradients of F F . For each of them define σ low ​ ( n ) ≔ n − c 1 n + c 1 ​ σ low , σ high ​ ( n ) ≔ n + c 1 n − c 1 ​ σ high . \sigma_{\textup{low}}(n)\coloneqq\frac{n-c_{1}}{n+c_{1}}\sigma_{\textup{low}}\kern 5.0pt,\quad\sigma_{\textup{high}}(n)\coloneqq\frac{n+c_{1}}{n-c_{1}}\sigma_{\textup{high}}\kern 5.0pt. If ( 2 ′ ) (2^{\prime}) is satisfied then also (2) is satisfied, because if 𝒓 t 0 ∈ S ⁡ ( n ) \boldsymbol{r}^{t_{0}}\in S(n) , then also 𝒓 t 0 + k ∈ S ⁡ ( n ) \boldsymbol{r}^{t_{0}+k}\in S(n) for any k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 , and so we can apply condition ( 2 ′ ) (2^{\prime}) at t = t 0 + k + ℓ t=t_{0}+k+\ell and by taking the conditional expectation we obtain the original condition (2). ( 49 ) holds whenever 𝒓 t 0 = 𝒓 t − ℓ ∈ S ⁡ ( n ) \boldsymbol{r}^{t_{0}}=\boldsymbol{r}^{t-\ell}\in S(n) . So, assuming that n &gt; c 1 n&gt;c_{1} and 𝒓 t 0 ∈ S ⁡ ( n ) \boldsymbol{r}^{t_{0}}\in S(n) , we obtain σ low ​ ( n ) \displaystyle\sigma_{\textup{low}}(n) = n − c 1 n + c 1 ​ σ low Now invoking Proposition D.7 with S in = S ⁡ ( n + 1 ) S_{\textup{in}}=S(n+1) and S out = S ⁡ ( n ) S_{\textup{out}}=S(n) , we obtain that if 𝒓 t 0 ∈ S ⁡ ( n + 1 ) \boldsymbol{r}^{t_{0}}\in S(n+1) then there exists c &gt; 0 c&gt;0 such that for all t ≥ t 0 t\geq t_{0} 𝔼 ⁡ [ F ⁡ ( 𝒓 t ) | 𝒓 t 0 ] − F ⋆ ≤ c ​ γ high ​ ( n ) t − t 0 , \mathbb{E}\left[F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t})\mathbin{|}\boldsymbol{r}^{t_{0}}\right]-F^{\star}\leq c\gamma_{\textup{high}}(n)^{t-t_{0}}\kern 5.0pt, where γ high ​ ( n ) ≔ 1 − σ low ​ ( n ) / | 𝒜 | \gamma_{\textup{high}}(n)\coloneqq 1-\sigma_{\textup{low}}(n)/\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert . Proposition D.13 now implies that S ⁡ ( n + 1 ) S(n+1) is reached with probability 1, so γ high ​ ( n ) \gamma_{\textup{high}}(n) is a valid upper bound on the local convergence rate. Since the optimization starts at a deterministic point 𝒓 0 = 𝟎 \boldsymbol{r}^{0}=\mathbf{0} , and the randomization is among a finite set of choices, there is only a finite set of allocation vectors 𝒓 t \boldsymbol{r}^{t} that can be reached at any given iteration t t . If the optimization problem is non-degenerate, then none of these allocations (for any t t ) are the actual minimizers of F F . In that case, Proposition D.7 yields that if 𝒓 t 0 ∈ S ⁡ ( n + 1 ) \boldsymbol{r}^{t_{0}}\in S(n+1) , and it has a non-zero probability of occurring, then for all t 1 ≥ t 0 t_{1}\geq t_{0} , there exists c &gt; 0 c&gt;0 such that for all t ≥ t 1 t\geq t_{1} 𝔼 ⁡ [ F ⁡ ( 𝒓 t ) | 𝒓 t 1 ] − F ⋆ ≥ c ​ γ low ​ ( n ) t − t 1 , \mathbb{E}\left[F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t})\mathbin{|}\boldsymbol{r}^{t_{1}}\right]-F^{\star}\geq c\gamma_{\textup{low}}(n)^{t-t_{1}}\kern 5.0pt, where γ low ​ ( n ) ≔ 1 − σ high ​ ( n ) / | 𝒜 | \gamma_{\textup{low}}(n)\coloneqq 1-\sigma_{\textup{high}}(n)/\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert . Since S ⁡ ( n + 1 ) S(n+1) is reached with probability 1, this means that any valid upper bound must be greater than γ low ​ ( n ) \gamma_{\textup{low}}(n) . The theorem now follows, because γ high ​ ( n ) → 1 − σ low / | 𝒜 | \gamma_{\textup{high}}(n)\to 1-\sigma_{\textup{low}}/\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert , and γ low → 1 − σ high / | 𝒜 | \gamma_{\textup{low}}\to 1-\sigma_{\textup{high}}/\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert , as n → ∞ n\to\infty . D.6 Supporting Propositions for Theorem D.8 Throughout the propositions below, let F F be a convex + function attaining a minimum. Let P P be a projection on 𝒢 ⁡ ( F ) \mathcal{G}(F) . Since F F attains a minimum, its gradient set includes zero, and therefore in Proposition A.7 we have 𝒂 = 𝟎 \boldsymbol{a}=\mathbf{0} . This means that F ⁡ ( 𝒓 ) = F ⁡ ( P ​ 𝒓 ) , F(\boldsymbol{r})=F(P\boldsymbol{r})\kern 5.0pt, so we can assume, without loss of generality, that 𝒓 ⋆ ∈ 𝒢 ⁡ ( F ) \boldsymbol{r}^{\star}\in\mathcal{G}(F) . Let H ⋆ ≔ ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝒓 ⋆ ) H^{\star}\coloneqq\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{r}^{\star}) . Finally, recall that S ⁡ ( F , λ ) S(F,\lambda) denotes a level set (see Definition D.4 ). Proposition D.10 . For any λ &gt; F ⋆ \lambda&gt;F^{\star} , S ⁡ ( F , λ ) = S 0 + 𝒢 ​ ( F ) ⟂ S(F,\lambda)=S_{0}+\mathcal{G}(F)^{\perp} where S 0 ⊆ 𝒢 ⁡ ( F ) S_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{G}(F) is compact. Proof. Since F ⁡ ( 𝒓 ) = F ⁡ ( P ​ 𝒓 ) F(\boldsymbol{r})=F(P\boldsymbol{r}) , any level set S S can be written as S = S 0 + 𝒢 ​ ( F ) ⟂ S=S_{0}+\mathcal{G}(F)^{\perp} , where S 0 = S ∩ 𝒢 ⁡ ( F ) S_{0}=S\cap\mathcal{G}(F) . Now if λ &gt; F ⋆ \lambda&gt;F^{\star} then S S is non-empty and closed and hence so is S 0 S_{0} . It remains to argue that it is bounded. By convexity + , F F is strictly convex on 𝒢 ⁡ ( F ) \mathcal{G}(F) , so the minimum of F F on the sphere { 𝒓 ∈ 𝒢 ⁡ ( F ) : ∥ 𝒓 − 𝒓 ⋆ ∥ = 1 } \{\boldsymbol{r}\in\mathcal{G}(F):\&gt;\lVert\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\star}\rVert=1\} must be some λ 1 &gt; F ⋆ \lambda_{1}&gt;F^{\star} . By convexity, F ⁡ ( 𝒓 ) − F ⋆ ≥ λ 1 ​ ∥ 𝒓 − 𝒓 ⋆ ∥ F(\boldsymbol{r})-F^{\star}\geq\lambda_{1}\lVert\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\star}\rVert for all 𝒓 ∈ 𝒢 ⁡ ( F ) \boldsymbol{r}\in\mathcal{G}(F) . Since S 0 ⊆ 𝒢 ⁡ ( F ) S_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{G}(F) and F ⁡ ( 𝒓 ) ≤ λ F(\boldsymbol{r})\leq\lambda for 𝒓 ∈ S 0 \boldsymbol{r}\in S_{0} , the set S 0 S_{0} must be bounded. ∎ Proposition D.11 . Let ε max &gt; 0 \varepsilon_{\max}&gt;0 . Then there exists a constant c &gt; 0 c&gt;0 such that for all 0 &lt; ε ≤ ε max 0&lt;\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{\max} , and all 𝐫 ∈ S ⁡ ( F , F ⋆ + ε 2 ) \boldsymbol{r}\in S(F,F^{\star}+\varepsilon^{2}) , we have ∥ P ​ 𝒓 − 𝒓 ⋆ ∥ ≤ c ​ ε , ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝒓 ) ≃ ( 1 ± c ​ ε ) ​ H ⋆ . \lVert P\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\star}\rVert\leq c\varepsilon\kern 5.0pt,\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{r})\simeq(1\pm c\varepsilon)H^{\star}\kern 5.0pt. Proof. Let S ≔ S ⁡ ( F , F ⋆ + ε max 2 ) S\coloneqq S(F,F^{\star}+\varepsilon_{\max}^{2}) and S 0 ≔ S ∩ 𝒢 ⁡ ( F ) S_{0}\coloneqq S\cap\mathcal{G}(F) , which is compact by Proposition D.10 . Let σ 1 ≔ min 𝒓 ∈ S 0 ⁡ λ min ​ ( ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝒓 ) , P ) . \sigma_{1}\coloneqq\min_{\boldsymbol{r}\in S_{0}}\lambda_{\min}(\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{r}),P)\kern 5.0pt. Note that λ min ​ ( ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝒓 ) , P ) &gt; 0 \lambda_{\min}(\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{r}),P)&gt;0 on the compact set S 0 S_{0} , and ∇ 2 F ​ ( ⋅ ) \nabla^{2}F(\cdot) and λ min ​ ( ⋅ , P ) \lambda_{\min}(\cdot,P) are continuous, so σ 1 &gt; 0 \sigma_{1}&gt;0 . Therefore, F F is strictly convex on S S with the strict convexity constant σ 1 \sigma_{1} . Using the fact that ∇ F ​ ( 𝒓 ⋆ ) = 𝟎 \nabla F(\boldsymbol{r}^{\star})=\mathbf{0} , we then have for any 𝒓 ∈ S \boldsymbol{r}\in S , F ⁡ ( 𝒓 ) = F ⁡ ( P ​ 𝒓 ) ≥ F ⋆ + 1 2 ​ σ 1 ​ ∥ P ​ 𝒓 − 𝒓 ⋆ ∥ 2 . \displaystyle F(\boldsymbol{r})=F(P\boldsymbol{r})\geq F^{\star}+\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{1}\lVert P\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\star}\rVert^{2}\kern 5.0pt. Therefore, if 𝒓 ∈ S ⁡ ( F , F ⋆ + ε 2 ) ⊆ S \boldsymbol{r}\in S(F,F^{\star}+\varepsilon^{2})\subseteq S then ∥ P ​ 𝒓 − 𝒓 ⋆ ∥ ≤ ε ​ 2 / σ 1 . \lVert P\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\star}\rVert\leq\varepsilon\sqrt{2/\sigma_{1}}\kern 5.0pt. For the bound on the Hessian, note that since the third derivative of F F is continuous, it is upper bounded on S 0 S_{0} . Therefore, the Hessian is Lipschitz with some constant L L on S 0 S_{0} , and so ∥ ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝒓 ) − H ⋆ ∥ ≤ L ⁡ ∥ P ​ 𝒓 − 𝒓 ⋆ ∥ \lVert\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{r})-H^{\star}\rVert\leq L\lVert P\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\star}\rVert . Thus, since range ⁡ ( ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝒓 ) − H ⋆ ) ⊆ 𝒢 ⁡ ( F ) \range(\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{r})-H^{\star})\subseteq\mathcal{G}(F) , we have ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝒓 ) ≃ H ⋆ ± L ⁡ ∥ P ​ 𝒓 − 𝒓 ⋆ ∥ ​ P . \nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{r})\simeq H^{\star}\pm L\lVert P\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\star}\rVert P\kern 5.0pt. Since σ 2 ​ P ⪯ H ⋆ \sigma_{2}P\preceq H^{\star} for some σ 2 &gt; 0 \sigma_{2}&gt;0 , we obtain ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝒓 ) ≃ ( 1 ± L ​ σ 2 − 1 ​ ∥ P ​ 𝒓 − 𝒓 ⋆ ∥ ) ​ H ⋆ . \nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{r})\simeq(1\pm L\sigma_{2}^{-1}\lVert P\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\star}\rVert)H^{\star}\kern 5.0pt. Thus, for 𝒓 ∈ S ⁡ ( F , F ⋆ + ε 2 ) ⊆ S \boldsymbol{r}\in S(F,F^{\star}+\varepsilon^{2})\subseteq S we have ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝒓 ) ≃ ( 1 ± ε ​ L ​ σ 2 − 1 ​ 2 / σ 1 ) ​ H ⋆ . \nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{r})\simeq(1\pm\varepsilon L\sigma_{2}^{-1}\sqrt{2/\sigma_{1}})H^{\star}\kern 5.0pt. Setting c ≔ max ⁡ { 2 / σ 1 , L ​ σ 2 − 1 ​ 2 / σ 1 } c\coloneqq\max\{\sqrt{2/\sigma_{1}},\,L\sigma_{2}^{-1}\sqrt{2/\sigma_{1}}\} then proves the proposition. ∎ Proposition D.12 . Let S S be a convex set and A A and B B positive-semidefinite matrices such that A ⪯ ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝐫 ) ⪯ B A\preceq\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{r})\preceq B for all 𝐫 ∈ S \boldsymbol{r}\in S . Then F F is strongly convex and smooth on S S with respect to A A and B B . Proof. Let 𝒓 ∈ S \boldsymbol{r}\in S , 𝒓 + 𝜹 ∈ S \boldsymbol{r}+\boldsymbol{\delta}\in S . The proposition now follows, because A ⪯ [ ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝒓 ′ ) ] ⪯ B A\preceq[\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime})]\preceq B . ∎ Proposition D.13 . For any λ &gt; F ⋆ \lambda&gt;F^{\star} , with probability 1, the randomized block-coordinate descent algorithm with the objective F F reaches an iteration t t in which 𝐫 t ∈ S ⁡ ( F , λ ) \boldsymbol{r}^{t}\in S(F,\lambda) . Proof. Since the proposition holds for λ ≥ F ⁡ ( 𝒓 0 ) \lambda\geq F(\boldsymbol{r}^{0}) , consider the case F ⋆ &lt; λ &lt; F ⁡ ( 𝒓 0 ) F^{\star}&lt;\lambda&lt;F(\boldsymbol{r}^{0}) , and in particular assume F ⋆ &lt; F ⁡ ( 𝒓 0 ) F^{\star}&lt;F(\boldsymbol{r}^{0}) . We prove the proposition by applying Theorem D.6 . Let S ≔ S ⁡ ( F , F ⁡ ( 𝒓 0 ) + 1 ) S\coloneqq S(F,F(\boldsymbol{r}^{0})+1) and S 0 ≔ S ∩ 𝒢 ⁡ ( F ) S_{0}\coloneqq S\cap\mathcal{G}(F) , which is compact by Proposition D.10 . Let c min ≔ min 𝒓 ∈ S 0 ⁡ λ min ​ ( ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝒓 ) , P ) , c max ≔ max 𝒓 ∈ S 0 ⁡ λ max ​ ( ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝒓 ) , P ) . c_{\min}\coloneqq\min_{\boldsymbol{r}\in S_{0}}\lambda_{\min}(\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{r}),P)\kern 5.0pt,\quad c_{\max}\coloneqq\max_{\boldsymbol{r}\in S_{0}}\lambda_{\max}(\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{r}),P)\kern 5.0pt. Similarly, since λ max ​ ( ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝒓 ) , P ) &lt; ∞ \lambda_{\max}(\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{r}),P)&lt;\infty on S 0 S_{0} , the continuity yields c max &lt; ∞ c_{\max}&lt;\infty . Since ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝒓 ) = ∇ 2 F ​ ( P ​ 𝒓 ) \nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{r})=\nabla^{2}F(P\boldsymbol{r}) , we have that for all 𝒓 ∈ S \boldsymbol{r}\in S c min ​ P ⪯ ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝒓 ) ⪯ c max ​ P . c_{\min}P\preceq\nabla^{2}F(\boldsymbol{r})\preceq c_{\max}P\kern 5.0pt. Therefore, by Proposition D.12 , F F is strongly convex and smooth on S S with respect to A ≔ c min ​ P A\coloneqq c_{\min}P and B ≔ c max ​ P B\coloneqq c_{\max}P . Let σ low ≔ λ min ​ ( 𝒟 ​ ( B ) + , A + ) = λ min ​ ( c max − 1 ​ 𝒟 ​ ( P ) + , c min − 1 ​ P ) = c min c max ⋅ λ min ​ ( 𝒟 ​ ( P ) + , P ) , \sigma_{\textup{low}}\coloneqq\lambda_{\min}\bigl(\mathcal{D}(B)^{+},\,A^{+}\bigr)=\lambda_{\min}\bigl(c_{\max}^{-1}\mathcal{D}(P)^{+},\,c_{\min}^{-1}P\bigr)=\frac{c_{\min}}{c_{\max}}\cdot\lambda_{\min}(\mathcal{D}(P)^{+},\,P)\kern 5.0pt, which is positive, because range ⁡ ( P ) ⊆ range ⁡ ( 𝒟 ⁡ ( P ) ) \range(P)\subseteq\range(\mathcal{D}(P)) by Proposition D.9 . Now, by Proposition D.7 , with S in = S ⁡ ( F , F ⁡ ( 𝒓 0 ) ) S_{\textup{in}}=S(F,F(\boldsymbol{r}^{0})) , S out = S ⁡ ( F , F ⁡ ( 𝒓 0 ) + 1 ) = S S_{\textup{out}}=S(F,F(\boldsymbol{r}^{0})+1)=S , and the above matrices A A and B B , the scalar σ low \sigma_{\textup{low}} , and ℓ = 0 \ell=0 , we obtain that for some constant c c and γ ≔ ( 1 − σ low / | 𝒜 | ) &lt; 1 \gamma\coloneqq(1-\sigma_{\textup{low}}/\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert)&lt;1 , 𝔼 ⁡ [ F ⁡ ( 𝒓 t ) ] − F ⋆ = 𝔼 ⁡ [ F ⁡ ( 𝒓 t ) | 𝒓 0 ] − F ⋆ ≤ c ​ γ t . \mathbb{E}\left[F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t})\right]-F^{\star}=\mathbb{E}\left[F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t})\mathbin{|}\boldsymbol{r}^{0}\right]-F^{\star}\leq c\gamma^{t}\kern 5.0pt. To finish the proof, we will appeal to Borel-Cantelli lemma and show that the algorithm must reach S ⁡ ( F , λ ) S(F,\lambda) with probability 1. Specifically, note that by the Markov inequality ∑ t = 1 ∞ ℙ { F ( 𝒓 t ) ≥ λ } \displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left\{F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t})\geq\lambda\right\} = ∑ t = 1 ∞ ℙ { F ( 𝒓 t ) − F ⋆ ≥ λ − F ⋆ } \displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left\{F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t})-F^{\star}\geq\lambda-F^{\star}\right\} ≤ ∑ t = 1 ∞ c ​ γ t λ − F ⋆ = c ​ γ ( 1 − γ ) ​ ( λ − F ⋆ ) &lt; ∞ , \displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\frac{c\gamma^{t}}{\lambda-F^{\star}}=\frac{c\gamma}{(1-\gamma)(\lambda-F^{\star})}&lt;\infty\kern 5.0pt, so with probability 1, only a finite number of the events { F ( 𝒓 t ) ≥ λ } \{F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t})\geq\lambda\} will occur; in other words, the level set S ⁡ ( F , λ ) S(F,\lambda) is reached with probability 1. ∎ D.7 Local Convergence of the Market Throughout this section, we assume that C C is convex + , which implies that F F is convex + as well. Our key tool for the analysis of the convergence error of the market is Theorem D.8 . Therefore, we need to analyze the gradient and Hessian of F F . The matrices M 1 M_{1} and M 2 M_{2} in the statement of the theorem do not depend on the liquidity parameter b b . The matrix M 2 M_{2} , which is the dominant part of the Hessian pseudoinverse ( H ⋆ ) + (H^{\star})^{+} , is also independent of the trader dynamics and the cost function. On the other hand, the matrix M 1 M_{1} reflects the cost function and dynamics. The pseudoinverse 𝒟 ​ ( H ⋆ ) + \mathcal{D}(H^{\star})^{+} approximately equals b ​ M 1 bM_{1} as b → 0 b\to 0 . The main implication is that σ low = Ω ⁡ ( b ) \sigma_{\textup{low}}=\Omega(b) . This yields linear convergence rate bound γ high = 1 − Ω ⁡ ( b ) \gamma_{\textup{high}}=1-\Omega(b) , which suggests worse convergence as b → 0 b\to 0 . However, in the absence of a matching lower bound, we cannot conclude that the actual convergence gets worse as b → 0 b\to 0 . In Appendix D.7.1 , we derive a matching bound σ high = O ⁡ ( b ) \sigma_{\textup{high}}=O(b) for ASD . Thus, for ASD , it is not possible to achieve a linear convergence rate better than 1 − Θ ⁡ ( b ) 1-\Theta(b) . (We conjecture similar behavior for SSD .) This means there is a tradeoff between convergence, which slows down as b → 0 b\to 0 , and the market-maker bias, which gets smaller. Proof of Theorem D.15 . In our tighter analysis of ASD , we derive a tighter bound on the expected curvature, exploiting the fact that the updates are chosen uniformly at random. While our analysis only applies to ASD , we conjecture that a similar style of analysis can also work for SSD . We will index blocks by i i rather than α \alpha , since each block consists of all the coordinates controlled by the trader i i . We begin by a detailed analysis of how the block-coordinate updates affect the value of the gradient. Let 𝒓 \boldsymbol{r} by the current iterate. Consider the update Ψ i \Psi_{i} , which optimizes over the coordinates controlled by trader i i (see Eq. 48 ), and let 𝒓 ′ = Ψ i ​ ( 𝒓 ) \boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}=\Psi_{i}(\boldsymbol{r}) be the new iterate. By the optimality of the update, we have 𝝁 i ​ ( 𝒓 ′ ) = ∇ F i ​ ( − 𝒓 i ′ ) = ∇ C b ​ ( ∑ j 𝒓 j ′ ) = 𝝁 0 ​ ( 𝒓 ′ ) \boldsymbol{\mu}_{i}(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime})=\nabla F_{i}(-\boldsymbol{r}_{i}^{\prime})=\nabla C_{b}\bigl(\textstyle\sum_{j}\boldsymbol{r}_{j}^{\prime}\bigr)=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}) and therefore, by Eq. ( 65 ), for all j ∈ [ N ] j\in[N] , ∇ j F ​ ( 𝒓 ′ ) = − 𝝁 j ​ ( 𝒓 ′ ) + 𝝁 0 ​ ( 𝒓 ′ ) = − 𝝁 j ​ ( 𝒓 ′ ) + 𝝁 i ​ ( 𝒓 ′ ) . \nabla_{j}F(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime})=-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime})+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime})=-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime})+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{i}(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime})\kern 5.0pt. Thus, after the first update, the gradient ∇ F ​ ( 𝒓 ) \nabla F(\boldsymbol{r}) can be expressed using pairwise differences of 𝝁 j ​ ( 𝒓 ) \boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}(\boldsymbol{r}) . When the update Ψ i \Psi_{i} is performed, the value of 𝝁 i \boldsymbol{\mu}_{i} changes, whereas 𝝁 j \boldsymbol{\mu}_{j} for j ≠ i j\neq i is unchanged. The amount of change in 𝝁 i \boldsymbol{\mu}_{i} will be denoted as 𝜹 i \boldsymbol{\delta}_{i} : 𝜹 i ​ ( 𝒓 ) ≔ 𝝁 i ​ ( 𝒓 ′ ) − 𝝁 i ​ ( 𝒓 ) ​ where ​ 𝒓 ′ = Ψ i ​ ( 𝒓 ) . \boldsymbol{\delta}_{i}(\boldsymbol{r})\coloneqq\boldsymbol{\mu}_{i}(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime})-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{i}(\boldsymbol{r})\text{ where }\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}=\Psi_{i}(\boldsymbol{r})\kern 5.0pt. This is locally bounded as follows: Lemma D.16 . There exists constants b 0 , c &gt; 0 b_{0},c&gt;0 such that for every b ≤ b 0 b\leq b_{0} there exists a proper level set S S such that if 𝐫 ∈ S \boldsymbol{r}\in S then ‖ 𝜹 i ( 𝒓 ) ‖ ≤ c b ∥ 𝝁 i ( 𝒓 ) − 𝝁 0 ( 𝒓 ) ∥ \left\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}_{i}(\boldsymbol{r})\right\rVert\leq cb\bigl\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}_{i}(\boldsymbol{r})-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}(\boldsymbol{r})\bigr\rVert for all i ∈ [ N ] i\in[N] . Consider the all-securities dynamics. Let M 1 ′ ≔ P ⊗ H C + M_{1}^{\prime}\coloneqq P\otimes H_{C}^{+} and M 2 ≔ ( P ​ D ​ P ) + ⊗ H T + M_{2}\coloneqq(PDP)^{+}\otimes H_{T}^{+} . Then for every sufficiently small b b , there exists a proper level set S S such that 𝔼 ⁡ [ ( 𝒈 t + 1 ) ⊺ ​ 𝒟 ​ ( H ⋆ ) + ​ 𝒈 t + 1 | 𝒓 t ] \displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[(\boldsymbol{g}^{t+1})^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}(H^{\star})^{+}\boldsymbol{g}^{t+1}\mathbin{\bigm|}\boldsymbol{r}^{t}\right] ≃ ( 𝒈 t ) ⊺ ​ ( ( 1 ± O ⁡ ( b ) ) ⋅ 2 ​ b ​ M 1 ′ ) ​ 𝒈 t \displaystyle\simeq(\boldsymbol{g}^{t})^{\intercal}\bigl(\;\left(1\pm O(b)\right)\cdot 2bM_{1}^{\prime}\;\bigr)\boldsymbol{g}^{t} (83) 𝔼 ⁡ [ ( 𝒈 t + 1 ) ⊺ ​ ( H ⋆ ) + ​ 𝒈 t + 1 | 𝒓 t ] \displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[(\boldsymbol{g}^{t+1})^{\intercal}(H^{\star})^{+}\boldsymbol{g}^{t+1}\mathbin{\bigm|}\boldsymbol{r}^{t}\right] ≃ ( 𝒈 t ) ⊺ ​ ( M 2 ± O ⁡ ( b ) ​ M 1 ′ ) ​ 𝒈 t \displaystyle\simeq(\boldsymbol{g}^{t})^{\intercal}\bigl(\;M_{2}\pm O(b)M_{1}^{\prime}\;\bigr)\boldsymbol{g}^{t} (84) whenever 𝐫 t − 1 ∈ S \boldsymbol{r}^{t-1}\in S . Local strong convexity is bounded from below and above by σ low \displaystyle\sigma_{\textup{low}} = 2 ​ b ⋅ λ min ​ ( P ​ D ​ P ) ⋅ λ min ​ ( H T 1 / 2 ​ H C + ​ H T 1 / 2 ) − O ⁡ ( b 2 ) \displaystyle=2b\cdot\lambda_{\min}(PDP)\cdot\lambda_{\min}\bigl(\;H_{T}^{1/2}H_{C}^{+}H_{T}^{1/2}\;\bigr)-O(b^{2}) σ high \displaystyle\sigma_{\textup{high}} = 2 ​ b ⋅ λ max ​ ( P ​ D ​ P ) ⋅ λ max ​ ( H T 1 / 2 ​ H C + ​ H T 1 / 2 ) + O ⁡ ( b 2 ) , \displaystyle=2b\cdot\lambda_{\max}(PDP)\cdot\lambda_{\max}\bigl(\;H_{T}^{1/2}H_{C}^{+}H_{T}^{1/2}\;\bigr)+O(b^{2})\kern 5.0pt, where λ min ​ ( ⋅ ) \lambda_{\min}(\cdot) and λ max ​ ( ⋅ ) \lambda_{\max}(\cdot) denote the smallest and the largest positive eigenvalue of a matrix. As mentioned above, the key consequence of Theorem D.17 is the fact that ASD converges at the rate 1 − Θ ⁡ ( b ) 1-\Theta(b) . The key difference from Theorem D.15 is in the expression for 𝒟 ​ ( H ⋆ ) + \mathcal{D}(H^{\star})^{+} . While 𝒟 ​ ( H ⋆ ) + ≈ b ⁡ ( I N ⊗ H C + ) \mathcal{D}(H^{\star})^{+}\approx b(I_{N}\otimes H_{C}^{+}) , as stated in Theorem D.15 , when we take an expectation over an update in a single iteration, the action of 𝒟 ​ ( H ⋆ ) + \mathcal{D}(H^{\star})^{+} is equivalent to that of the matrix 2 ​ b ​ ( P ⊗ H C + ) 2b(P\otimes H_{C}^{+}) . Thus, the averaging effect of an expectation is to remove one rank from matrix I N I_{N} and replace it by the centering matrix P = I N − 𝟏𝟏 ⊺ / N P=I_{N}-\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\intercal}/N (while incurring an extra factor of two). This has two consequences. First, the lower bound σ low \sigma_{\textup{low}} is a factor of two larger. Second, we can now obtain a non-trivial upper bound σ high \sigma_{\textup{high}} , which would not be possible via an analog of Eq. ( 50 ), because the range of 𝒟 ​ ( H ⋆ ) + \mathcal{D}(H^{\star})^{+} is too large. Proof of Theorem D.17 . Consider b 0 b_{0} and c c from Lemma D.16 , and let b ≤ b 0 b\leq b_{0} and S S be the level set from the lemma. Assume that 𝒓 t − 1 ∈ S \boldsymbol{r}^{t-1}\in S . After the update in the iteration t − 1 t-1 , it is guaranteed that 𝝁 0 ​ ( 𝒓 t ) = 𝝁 j ​ ( 𝒓 t ) \boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}(\boldsymbol{r}^{t})=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}(\boldsymbol{r}^{t}) for some j j . We analyze the update in the following iteration, i.e., the iteration t t . We write 𝒓 \boldsymbol{r} for 𝒓 t \boldsymbol{r}^{t} and 𝒓 ′ \boldsymbol{r}^{\prime} for 𝒓 t + 1 \boldsymbol{r}^{t+1} . Let 𝒈 ≔ ∇ F ​ ( 𝒓 ) \boldsymbol{g}\coloneqq\nabla F(\boldsymbol{r}) , 𝝁 j ≔ 𝝁 j ​ ( 𝒓 ) \boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}\coloneqq\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}(\boldsymbol{r}) for j ∈ [ N ] j\in[N] , 𝝁 0 ≔ 𝝁 0 ​ ( 𝒓 ) \boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}\coloneqq\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}(\boldsymbol{r}) , and similarly define 𝒈 ′ \boldsymbol{g}^{\prime} , 𝝁 j ′ \boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}_{j} , 𝝁 0 ′ \boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}_{0} for the iterate 𝒓 ′ \boldsymbol{r}^{\prime} . Recall from Eq. ( 65 ) that the blocks of the gradient are 𝒈 j = 𝝁 j − 𝝁 0 . \boldsymbol{g}_{j}=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}\kern 5.0pt. Also recall that P = I N − 𝟏𝟏 ⊺ / N P=I_{N}-\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\intercal}/N . The theorem now follows, because λ min ​ ( M 1 , M 2 ) \displaystyle\lambda_{\min}(M_{1},M_{2}) = λ min ​ ( ( M 2 + ) 1 / 2 ​ M 1 ​ ( M 2 + ) 1 / 2 ) \displaystyle=\lambda_{\min}\bigl(\;(M_{2}^{+})^{1/2}M_{1}(M_{2}^{+})^{1/2}\;\bigr) = λ min ​ ( P ​ D ​ P ) ⋅ λ min ​ ( H T 1 / 2 ​ H C + ​ H T 1 / 2 ) , \displaystyle=\lambda_{\min}(PDP)\cdot\lambda_{\min}\bigl(\;H_{T}^{1/2}H_{C}^{+}H_{T}^{1/2}\;\bigr)\kern 5.0pt, and similarly for λ max ​ ( M 1 , M 2 ) \lambda_{\max}(M_{1},M_{2}) . ∎ D.7.2 Tighter Relationship between Suboptimality and Convergence Error for ASD For ASD , it is possible to establish a tighter relationship between the convergence error ∥ 𝝁 t − 𝝁 ⋆ ∥ \lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t}-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\star}\rVert and suboptimality F ⁡ ( 𝒓 t ) − F ⋆ F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t})-F^{\star} than we proved in Appendix D.2 . Specifically, we showed that ∥ 𝝁 t − 𝝁 ⋆ ∥ 2 = O ⁡ ( F ⁡ ( 𝒓 t ) − F ⋆ ) \lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t}-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\star}\rVert^{2}=O(F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t})-F^{\star}) for convex + C C . Under ASD , we obtain a matching lower bound for the one-step expectation, i.e., 𝔼 ⁡ [ ∥ 𝝁 t + 1 − 𝝁 ⋆ ∥ 2 | 𝒓 t ] = Θ ⁡ ( F ⁡ ( 𝒓 t ) − F ⋆ ) \mathbb{E}\left[\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t+1}-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\star}\rVert^{2}\mathbin{|}\boldsymbol{r}^{t}\right]=\Theta(F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t})-F^{\star}) . Note that, as before, we suppress the dependence on N N and a i a_{i} within the O ⁡ ( ⋅ ) O(\cdot) and Θ ⁡ ( ⋅ ) \Theta(\cdot) notation. ∎ D.7.3 Summary of Local Convergence Results Here we summarize our local convergence results for ASD and SSD . Recall that D ≔ diag i ∈ [ N ] ⁡ a i D\coloneqq\diag_{i\in[N]}a_{i} , P ≔ I N − 𝟏𝟏 ⊺ / N P\coloneqq I_{N}-\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\intercal}/N , and λ min ​ ( ⋅ ) \lambda_{\min}(\cdot) and λ max ​ ( ⋅ ) \lambda_{\max}(\cdot) to denote the smallest and the largest positive eigenvalues of a matrix. Theorem D.19 . Assume that C C is convex + . Let H T ≔ H T ​ ( 𝛍 ¯ ) H_{T}\coloneqq H_{T}(\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}) , H C ≔ H C ​ ( 𝛍 ¯ ) H_{C}\coloneqq H_{C}(\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}) , and D C D_{C} be the diagonal matrix with the diagonal of H C H_{C} . For all-securities dynamics, local strong convexity is bounded from below and above by σ low ASD \displaystyle\sigma_{\textup{low}}^{\texttt{{ASD}}} = 2 ​ b ⋅ λ min ​ ( P ​ D ​ P ) ⋅ λ min ​ ( H T 1 / 2 ​ H C + ​ H T 1 / 2 ) − O ⁡ ( b 2 ) , \displaystyle=2b\cdot\lambda_{\min}(PDP)\cdot\lambda_{\min}\bigl(\;H_{T}^{1/2}H_{C}^{+}H_{T}^{1/2}\;\bigr)-O(b^{2})\kern 5.0pt, σ high ASD \displaystyle\sigma_{\textup{high}}^{\texttt{{ASD}}} = 2 ​ b ⋅ λ max ​ ( P ​ D ​ P ) ⋅ λ max ​ ( H T 1 / 2 ​ H C + ​ H T 1 / 2 ) + O ⁡ ( b 2 ) . \displaystyle=2b\cdot\lambda_{\max}(PDP)\cdot\lambda_{\max}\bigl(\;H_{T}^{1/2}H_{C}^{+}H_{T}^{1/2}\;\bigr)+O(b^{2})\kern 5.0pt. For single-security dynamics, local strong convexity is bounded from below by σ low SSD \displaystyle\sigma_{\textup{low}}^{\texttt{{SSD}}} = b ⋅ λ min ​ ( P ​ D ​ P ) ⋅ λ min ​ ( H T 1 / 2 ​ D C + ​ H T 1 / 2 ) − O ⁡ ( b 2 ) . \displaystyle=b\cdot\lambda_{\min}(PDP)\cdot\lambda_{\min}\bigl(\;H_{T}^{1/2}D_{C}^{+}H_{T}^{1/2}\;\bigr)-O(b^{2})\kern 5.0pt. Proof. The theorem follows immediately from Theorems D.15 and D.17 . ∎ Recall that by Theorem D.8 , the bounds on local strong convexity translate into bounds on local convergence rate as γ high = 1 − σ low / N \gamma_{\textup{high}}=1-\sigma_{\textup{low}}/N and γ low = 1 − σ high / N \gamma_{\textup{low}}=1-\sigma_{\textup{high}}/N for ASD , and γ high = 1 − σ low / N ​ K \gamma_{\textup{high}}=1-\sigma_{\textup{low}}/NK for SSD . So, for ASD , Theorem D.19 proves linear convergence with the rate γ = 1 − Θ ⁡ ( b ) \gamma=1-\Theta(b) . This means that the convergence gets worse as b → 0 b\to 0 , leading to a trade-off with the bias, which decreases as b → 0 b\to 0 . Our numerical experiments in Section 7 and Appendix E show that these bounds on the convergence rate are empirically quite tight. Below, we show an example when the two bounds match except for the O ⁡ ( b 2 ) O(b^{2}) terms: when all traders have identical risk aversions and the cost function is LMSR . For SSD , we only present a lower bound on the local strong convexity, which suffices to establish a linear convergence rate. This bound is worse by a factor of two than the bound for ASD . This we believe is only an artifact of a looser analysis and we expect that the reasoning that gave rise to a tighter analysis of ASD can be generalized to SSD . Our experiments in Appendix E also suggest that our SSD analysis is looser than the ASD analysis. Example D.20 (Convergence of LMSR under ASD ) . We next demonstrate the tightness of our bounds for ASD . Consider the setting when N ≥ 2 N\geq 2 and the risk aversion of all traders equals a a . Then P ​ D ​ P = a ​ P ​ I N ​ P = a ​ P PDP=aPI_{N}P=aP , and since P P is a non-zero projection matrix, we obtain λ min ​ ( P ​ D ​ P ) = a ​ λ min ​ ( P ) = a \lambda_{\min}(PDP)=a\lambda_{\min}(P)=a and similarly λ max ​ ( P ​ D ​ P ) = a \lambda_{\max}(PDP)=a . Furthermore, if the cost is LMSR then H C = H T H_{C}=H_{T} , and thus H T 1 / 2 ​ H C + ​ H T 1 / 2 = I K − 𝟏𝟏 ⊺ / K H_{T}^{1/2}H_{C}^{+}H_{T}^{1/2}=I_{K}-\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\intercal}/K . Therefore, Theorem 6.2 yields the bounds σ low ASD = 2 ​ a ​ b − O ⁡ ( b 2 ) \sigma_{\textup{low}}^{\texttt{{ASD}}}=2ab-O(b^{2}) and σ high ASD = 2 ​ a ​ b + O ⁡ ( b 2 ) \sigma_{\textup{high}}^{\texttt{{ASD}}}=2ab+O(b^{2}) , whose main asymptotic terms match exactly. Thus, the objective decreases at the rate γ t \gamma^{t} and the convergence error at the rate γ t / 2 \gamma^{t/2} with γ = 1 − 2 ​ a ​ b / N + O ⁡ ( b 2 ) \gamma=1-2ab/N+O(b^{2}) , and the linear term in b b cannot be improved. D.8 Proof of Theorem 6.2 The theorem follows immediately from Theorem D.19 , because γ high = 1 − σ low / N \gamma_{\textup{high}}=1-\sigma_{\textup{low}}/N and γ low = 1 − σ high / N \gamma_{\textup{low}}=1-\sigma_{\textup{high}}/N for ASD (by Theorem D.8 ).
