Here we include experiments for an additional set of beliefs (uniform beliefs, defined below) and single-securities dynamics (defined in Appendix D.1 ). Once again, we consider a setting in which there is a complete market over K = 5 K=5 securities with N = 10 N=10 traders who have exponential utilities, exponential-family beliefs and risk aversion coefficients a i = 1 a_{i}=1 for i ∈ [ N ] i\in[N] . Similar to Section 7 , we fix the ground-truth natural parameter 𝜽 true \boldsymbol{\theta}^{\textup{true}} and independently sample the belief 𝜽 ~ i \boldsymbol{\tilde{\theta}}_{i} of each trader from Normal ​ ( 𝜽 true , σ 2 ​ I K ) \textup{Normal}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\textup{true}},\sigma^{2}I_{K}) . We consider two settings of the ground truth and beliefs: • Uniform Beliefs : All outcomes are equally likely. We set 𝜽 true = 𝟎 \boldsymbol{\theta}^{\textup{true}}=\boldsymbol{0} and σ = 1 \sigma=1 . • Single-Peaked Beliefs : One outcome is more likely than the others. Here we set θ 1 true = log ⁡ ( 1 − ν ⋅ ( k − 1 ) ) \theta^{\textup{true}}_{1}=\log(1-\nu\cdot(k-1)) and θ k true = log ⁡ ( ν ) \theta^{\textup{true}}_{k}=\log(\nu) for k ≠ 1 k\neq 1 . We use ν = 0.02 \nu=0.02 and σ = 5 \sigma=5 . Fig. 2 shows the trader beliefs and market-clearing equilibrium prices (calculated via Theorem 4.3 ) for both settings. Note that in Section 7 , we gave results for the case of single-peaked beliefs and all-security dynamics whereas here we present results for all four combinations of belief settings and trader dynamics. Figure 2: Two sets of beliefs of the N = 10 N=10 traders about the K = 5 K=5 outcomes. The beliefs were sampled once and then fixed in all experiments. The gray bars show the market-clearing equilibrium prices 𝝁 ¯ \bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}} as in Definition 3.1 and Theorem 4.3 . Bias/convergence tradeoffs We first examine the tradeoff that arises between market-maker bias and convergence error as the liquidity parameter of the market is adjusted. Since our main interest is in the effect of the cost function C C and liquidity parameter b b on error, we ignore the sources of error that do not depend on the choice of cost function, such as the sampling error. Fig. 3 shows the combined bias and convergence error, ∥ 𝝁 ¯ − 𝝁 t ​ ( b , C ) ∥ \lVert\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t}(b;C)\rVert , as a function of liquidity, for different beliefs and cost functions under ASD after different numbers of trades have occurred. (Other choices of norm lead to similar results.) Similarly, we give results for SSD in Fig. 4 . The minimum point on each curve tells us the optimal value of the liquidity parameter b b for the particular setting and number of trades. When the market has not been running long, larger values of b b lead to lower error. On the other hand, smaller values of b b are preferable as the number of trades grows, with the combined error approaching 0 for small b b . The combined error of LMSR is similar to that of the sum of independent LMSRs ( IND ) under uniform beliefs, but LMSR produces lower combined error for single-peaked beliefs. Figure 3: The tradeoff between marker-maker bias and convergence error for ASD with different beliefs and cost functions. Solid lines show the total bias and convergence error of LMSR after various numbers of trades, averaged over 20 random trade sequences. Dotted lines show the same for IND . Figure 4: The tradeoff between marker-maker bias and convergence error for SSD with different beliefs and cost functions. Solid lines show the total bias and convergence error of LMSR after various numbers of trades, averaged over 20 random trade sequences. Dotted lines show the same for IND . Market-maker bias We next focus in on the market-maker bias to empirically evaluate our bounds from Section 5 . From Theorem 5.6 , we know that ∥ 𝝁 ⋆ ​ ( b , C ) − 𝝁 ¯ ∥ ≈ b ⁡ ( a ¯ / N ) ​ ∥ H T ​ ( 𝝁 ¯ ) ​ ∂ C ∗ ​ ( 𝝁 ¯ ) ∥ . \lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\star}(b;C)-\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\rVert\approx b(\bar{a}/N)\lVert H_{T}(\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\partial C^{*}(\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\rVert. In Fig. 5 , we plot the empirical bias ∥ 𝝁 ⋆ ​ ( b , C ) − 𝝁 ¯ ∥ \lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\star}(b;C)-\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\rVert as a function of b b for both LMSR and IND under uniform and single-peaked beliefs, and in each case compare this bias with the approximation implied by the theory. We find that although Theorem 5.6 only gives an asymptotic guarantee as b → 0 b\to 0 , the approximation above is fairly accurate even for moderate values of b b . As Theorem 5.7 shows, the bias of IND is higher than that of LMSR at any fixed value of b b , but by no more than a factor of two. The difference is greater for single-peaked beliefs than uniform beliefs. Note that the bias is unaffected by the choice of trader dynamics. Figure 5: Market-maker bias as a function of b b for different beliefs and cost functions. Convergence error Finally, we turn to the convergence error. We first show that the local linear convergence rate kicks in very quickly—essentially from the start of trade in our simulations. We then examine the tightness of our bounds on the local convergence rate from Section 6 . From Theorem D.3 , we know that F ⁡ ( 𝒓 t ) − F ⋆ F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t})-F^{\star} is an upper bound on ∥ 𝝁 t − 𝝁 ⋆ ∥ 2 \lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t}-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\star}\rVert^{2} , and under ASD we also have F ⁡ ( 𝒓 t ) − F ⋆ = Θ ⁡ ( ∥ 𝝁 t − 𝝁 ⋆ ∥ 2 ) F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t})-F^{\star}=\Theta(\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t}-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\star}\rVert^{2}) (by Theorem D.18 ), where we suppress the implicit dependence on C C and b b . Rather than examining the convergence of prices directly, we examine convergence of the objective, which will be more convenient in the discussion below. Fig. 6 shows the empirical value of 𝔼 ^ ​ [ F ⁡ ( 𝒓 t ) ] − F ⋆ \mathbb{\hat{E}}\left[F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t})\right]-F^{\star} , where the expectation is the empirical average over the 20 random sequences, as a function of the number of trades, plotted on a log scale, for our two belief sets and cost functions under the all-securities trade dynamics. In all settings, the log of convergence error appears linear, matching the local asymptotic analysis in Section 6 . In other words, there exist some c ^ \hat{c} and γ ^ \hat{\gamma} such that, empirically, we have for all t t , 𝔼 ^ ​ [ F ⁡ ( 𝒓 t ) ] − F ⋆ ≈ c ^ ​ γ ^ t . \mathbb{\hat{E}}\left[F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t})\right]-F^{\star}\approx\hat{c}\hat{\gamma}^{t}. To examine the tightness of the bounds from Section 6 , we dig more deeply into the value of this empirical constant γ ^ \hat{\gamma} , which depends on C C and b b . Since this approximation holds for any sufficiently large t t , we can define γ ^ \hat{\gamma} by choosing some t 1 t_{1} and t 2 t_{2} and setting γ ^ = ( 𝔼 ^ ​ [ F ⁡ ( 𝒓 t 2 ) ] − F ⋆ 𝔼 ^ ​ [ F ⁡ ( 𝒓 t 1 ) ] − F ⋆ ) 1 / ( t 2 − t 1 ) . \hat{\gamma}=\left(\frac{\mathbb{\hat{E}}\left[F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t_{2}})\right]-F^{\star}}{\mathbb{\hat{E}}\left[F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t_{1}})\right]-F^{\star}}\right)^{1/(t_{2}-t_{1})}. If γ ^ \hat{\gamma} is the correct asymptotic convergence rate, then from Theorem D.8 , we should have 1 − σ high / N ≤ γ ^ ≤ 1 − σ low / N 1-\sigma_{\textup{high}}/N\leq\hat{\gamma}\leq 1-\sigma_{\textup{low}}/N for values of σ high \sigma_{\textup{high}} and σ low \sigma_{\textup{low}} that satisfy Eq. ( 49 ), since | 𝒜 | = N |\mathcal{A}|=N for ASD . Rearranging terms, we would expect that, for sufficiently large t 1 t_{1} and t 2 t_{2} , σ low ≤ N ⁡ ( 1 − ( 𝔼 ^ ​ [ F ⁡ ( 𝒓 t 2 ) ] − F ⋆ 𝔼 ^ ​ [ F ⁡ ( 𝒓 t 1 ) ] − F ⋆ ) 1 / ( t 2 − t 1 ) ) ≤ σ high . \sigma_{\textup{low}}\leq N\left(1-\left(\frac{\mathbb{\hat{E}}\left[F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t_{2}})\right]-F^{\star}}{\mathbb{\hat{E}}\left[F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t_{1}})\right]-F^{\star}}\right)^{1/(t_{2}-t_{1})}\right)\leq\sigma_{\textup{high}}. (118) We refer to this quantity that is upper and lower bounded by σ high \sigma_{\textup{high}} and σ low \sigma_{\textup{low}} as the empirical strong convexity σ ^ \hat{\sigma} . Note that σ low \sigma_{\textup{low}} and σ high \sigma_{\textup{high}} implicitly depend on b b and C C . Solid lines show the log error in objective for LMSR , dotted lines for IND . Figure 7: The empirical strong convexity from Eq. ( 118 ) under ASD for various values of t 1 , t 2 t_{1},t_{2} represented as dots, and asymptotic bounds for σ low \sigma_{\textup{low}} and σ high \sigma_{\textup{high}} from Theorem D.19 (ignoring O ⁡ ( b 2 ) O(b^{2}) terms) represented as lines. We can now check how well our theoretical lower and upper bounds on local strong convexity bound the empirical strong convexity σ ^ \hat{\sigma} . In Fig. 7 , we plot σ ^ \hat{\sigma} as a function of b b using different values of t 1 t_{1} and t 2 t_{2} and compare it with the asymptotic bounds of σ low \sigma_{\textup{low}} and σ high \sigma_{\textup{high}} computed as in Theorem D.19 , dropping the terms that are O ⁡ ( b 2 ) O(b^{2}) . We would expect to see σ low ≤ σ ^ ≤ σ high \sigma_{\textup{low}}\leq\hat{\sigma}\leq\sigma_{\textup{high}} as b b goes to 0, and indeed this is the case. For LMSR , the values of σ high \sigma_{\textup{high}} and σ low \sigma_{\textup{low}} coincide, and the empirical values for σ ^ \hat{\sigma} agree for small b b . We now turn to our results for single-security dynamics ( SSD ). Fig. 8 shows the empirical value of F ⁡ ( 𝒓 t ) − F ⋆ F(\boldsymbol{r}^{t})-F^{\star} , averaged over 20 random sequences of trade, as a function of the number of trades, plotted on a log scale, for our two belief sets and cost functions under SSD . The plots show the convergence error for LMSR and IND right on top of each other, suggesting that the main asymptotic term is driven by the diagonal of H C ​ ( 𝝁 ¯ ) H_{C}(\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}) , which is the same for both costs, and which appears in the lower bound of Theorem D.19 (with a multiplier that could be possibly improved). Similar to ASD , we also evaluate the empirical strong convexity σ ^ \hat{\sigma} . In this case, we only have access to a lower bound (Theorem D.19 ), which our plots show to be a valid albeit a somewhat loose bound. All the bounds that we used in the ASD and SSD strong convexity plots are summarized in Table 1 .
